> By the passing of time! Truly, man is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds, and urge one another to the truth, and urge one another to patience.

In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful

## Preface

People are driven by their beliefs more than by their ideas. For this reason religions will continue to hold the reins of the movement of human history and of human conflict, and the craft of theological discourse, dialogue and debate about religion will remain for as long as man exists on earth.

It falls to the scholars of this craft, those distinguished by a mind that seeks what is right and true and by a will that seeks the good and the happiness of humankind, to distinguish truth from illusion and right from wrong in the beliefs and opinions that human beings hold. Man's salvation and success depend on their freeing themselves from caprice and on the sincerity of their intentions in guiding man to the truth and the good.

I sincerely pray to God that my motive and purpose in what I have written be to contribute to achieving this aim, which is the aim of every true religion.


{8}------------------------------------------------



{9}------------------------------------------------

## Preliminary Remarks

Before presenting the subject of our study in detail, we would like to mention some matters that we find helpful in clarifying the subject of the research:

### First: The meaning of *milal* and *nihal*

*Al-milal* is the plural of *milla*; *milla*, with a *kasra* under the *mim*, means religion.
*Al-nihal* is the plural of *nihla*; *nihla*, with a *kasra* under the *nun*, means a claim. One says *intahala fulan al-shay'*, meaning that someone claimed a thing for himself.

### Second: The subject of the research and its method

The subject of our research concerns the great heavenly religions which, to distinguish between them, have conventionally been called Judaism, Christianity and Islam. Our aim is to distinguish within them between the Word of God as revelation expressed it and the word of man that was claimed to be from revelation or an expression of revelation; that is, between the *milla* (religion as God revealed it), as religion from God, and the *nihla* (a claim or doctrine put forward by human beings), as a claim made by human beings.

Our method in studying them will be historical and comparative. For, as Dr. al-Bahi says, the position of any community towards a creed it has embraced "appears in two forms: the form of strong faith,
and then the form of understanding and reasoning.

And the more the warmth of faith in hearts cools, the more the aspect of disagreement in understanding it comes to the fore" (1).

---

(1) Al-Janib al-Ilahi fi al-Tafkir al-Islami (The Divine Aspect in Islamic Thought), p. 54, Dar al-Kitab al-Arabi, Cairo 1967.

{10}------------------------------------------------

For us, the first form represents the three religions in the form in which revelation brought them down.

The second form, for us, represents the opinions and schools of thought that the followers of each of the three religions put forward, relying in what they held on rational interpretation and personal understanding of their religious texts.

With regard to Islam, we can easily and readily distinguish the *milla*, or religion, within it from the *nihla*, or claim, since its religious texts, foremost among them the Qur'an, are a truthful witness to both matters together: I mean Islam as revelation brought it down and Islam as its followers understood it. This enables us to uncover or critique the claims made by those who believe in it, and to measure how far they stand from revelation or how close to it.

As for Judaism and Christianity, the matter is quite different, since it is difficult, if not impossible, to distinguish religion from *nihla* in them, because the alteration of their sacred books has been established scientifically. In the field of precise scholarly documentation one cannot therefore rely on those books to distinguish the religion as revelation brought it down from the claims that human beings added to Judaism or Christianity in their present state.

Hence we have relied on the Qur'an, wherever possible, as a criterion of critique and distinction with regard to Judaism and Christianity. This position of ours does not stem from partisanship but from a purely scholarly motive, since the Qur'an has been subjected to scholarly criticism, of both its text and its chain of transmission, by the most fanatical of its opponents, and no one has been able to impugn its truthfulness and soundness in any of the issues it has raised, including those connected with the other religions. From a purely scholarly standpoint, then, we need to rely on it, not only in criticising the documents of those religions and bringing to light the gaps that occurred in them, but also to establish their very historical existence. Otherwise it would become scientifically easy to cast doubt on their heavenly lineage altogether, and the mantle of sanctity would fall from their documents and their symbols. The Gospels, for example, cannot by themselves, from a scholarly point of view, prove the existence of Jesus (peace be upon him); rather, they acquire their legitimacy and recognition from the existence of Jesus (peace be upon him) being proved by other routes and evidence. For leafing through the Gospels, as Charles Guignebert says, "is enough to convince us that their authors arrived at clearly contradictory arrangements of the same events and sayings, which compels us to say that they did not seek the actual truth and were not inspired by a fixed history whose sequence of events imposed itself upon them. On the contrary,

{11}------------------------------------------------

each of them followed his own inclination and his own plan in arranging and ordering his work .. and so we can no longer clearly distinguish the historical aspects of the person of Jesus, nor do we any longer possess the sources necessary to determine the events of his life with precision"<sup>(2)</sup>. The matter is no different with regard to Judaism. Both, then, are in need of the Qur'an, which grants them recognition of their lineage; relying on their own documents alone would be nothing but a gamble with their fate and with the legitimacy of their lineage.

### Third: The origin of the names

By this we mean answering the question: are the names of the three religions human or divine in origin?

Let us first know that the true religions sent down from God Almighty upon the messengers to guide human beings to the straight path are one in their foundations and one in their source, which is God, glory be to Him, the Exalted. All of them mean submission and complete compliance, in heart, mind and body, to everything God has commanded through His messengers. Revelation expressed this stance, or God named it, with a single name, in His words:

> - Truly, the religion in the sight of God is Islam (3)
> - Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him (4)
> - and I have chosen Islam for you as your religion (5)
> - Whomever God wills to guide, He opens his breast to Islam (6)
> - and I have been commanded to submit to the Lord of the worlds (7)
> - And who is better in speech than one who calls to God, does righteous deeds and says: I am of those who submit (the Muslims) (8)

(2) Al-Masihiyya: Nash'atuha wa Tatawwuruha (Christianity: Its Origin and Development), pp. 28-29, translated by Abd al-Halim Mahmud, al-Maktaba al-Asriyya, Beirut.

(3) Al Imran 3:19. (5) Al-Ma'ida 5:3.
(4) Al Imran 3:85. (6) Al-An'am 6:125.

{12}------------------------------------------------

It is revelation, then, that named the true religion Islam, and it is the religion with which all the prophets were charged and which they were commanded to convey to the people.

Thus of Abraham, the father of the prophets (peace be upon him), the Qur'an says:

> Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; rather, he was a man of pure faith, one who submitted (a Muslim) (9)

> And who would turn away from the *milla* of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself? We chose him in this world, and in the Hereafter he is among the righteous. When his Lord said to him, "Submit," he said, "I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds" (10)

And Islam is the religion that Abraham enjoined upon his sons. Together with his son Ishmael (peace be upon him) he prays:

> Our Lord, make us submissive to You, and of our descendants a nation submissive to You (11)

Likewise:

> And Abraham enjoined it upon his sons, and so did Jacob: "My sons, God has chosen for you the religion, so do not die except as those who submit (Muslims)" (12)

Then it is the testament of Jacob to his sons after him:

> Or were you witnesses when death came to Jacob, when he said to his sons, "What will you worship after me?" They said, "We will worship your God and the God

(9) Al Imran 3:67.
(10) Al-Baqara 2:130-131.
(11) Al-Baqara 2:12[8].
(12) Al-Baqara 2:132.

{13}------------------------------------------------

> of your fathers, Abraham, Ishmael and Isaac, One God, and to Him we submit (we are Muslims)" (13)

Then it is the religion of Moses, which he was charged to convey to the Children of Israel:

> And Moses said, "O my people, if you have believed in God, then put your trust in Him, if you are those who submit (Muslims)" (14)

And Moses' Pharaoh:

> Until, when drowning overtook him, he said, "I believe that there is no god but He in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am of those who submit (the Muslims)" (15)

He did not say "I am of the Jews" or "of the Israelites", because he knew that the religion to which Moses (peace be upon him) was calling was named Islam.

And Islam is the religion to which Solomon (peace be upon him) called the people of Sheba when he said to them:

> Do not exalt yourselves against me, but come to me in submission (as Muslims) (16)

And it is the religion of Jesus (peace be upon him), who:

> said, "Who are my helpers for God?" The disciples said, "We are God's helpers. We believe in God; bear witness that we submit (that we are Muslims)" (17)

(13) Al-Baqara 2:133.

(14) Yunus 10:84.

(15) Yunus 10:90.

(16) Al-Naml 27:31.

(17) Al Imran 3:52.

{14}------------------------------------------------

And finally it is the comprehensive message with which Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him) was charged and which he was commanded to convey to all people:

> Say: We believe in God and in what has been sent down to us, and in what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes, and in what was given to Moses and Jesus and the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit (we are Muslims) (18)

From all this it becomes clear that naming the true religion Islam is a divine naming, not a human one.

And Islam is the religion with which all the messengers were charged, from Adam to Muhammad, God's blessings and peace be upon them all, although it differed in its general or particular scope: with the earlier messengers it was a call specific to a particular time, place and people, and it culminated in a general call encompassing all people at the hands of Muhammad, God bless him, the Seal of the prophets and messengers.

As for Judaism or Christianity, these are names devised by human beings, originating in personal affiliation.

For this reason we find the Noble Qur'an at times mentioning them in the context of praise, when they believe, and at times in the context of censure, when they disbelieve; that is, it described them with both disbelief and belief.

In the context of praise, for example, are the words of the Exalted:

> Truly those who believe, and those who are Jews, and the Christians and the Sabians, whoever believes in God and the Last Day and does righteous deeds, they shall have their reward with their Lord, and no fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve (19)

(19) Al-Baqara 2:62.

(18) Al Imran 3:84-85.

{15}------------------------------------------------

In the context of censure, for example, are the words of the Exalted:

> The Jews say, "Ezra is the son of God," and the Christians say, "The Messiah is the son of God." That is their saying with their mouths; they imitate the saying of those who disbelieved before. May God fight them; how are they turned away! They have taken their rabbis and their monks as lords besides God, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were commanded only to worship One God; there is no god but He. Glory be to Him above what they associate with Him (20)

Islam, however, it mentions only in the context of praise, because its opposite is disbelief or associating partners with God.

We conclude from this that Judaism, and Christianity, which is also called *al-Nasraniyya*, are conventional names applied to the followers of Moses and Jesus (peace be upon them), and they do not necessarily mean that these followers adhere to the true religion that was sent down to Moses or Jesus, which God named Islam.

(20) Al-Tawba 9:30-31.

Chapter One
Judaism

{18}------------------------------------------------



{19}------------------------------------------------

## Its Origin and Development

The Jews are the followers of Moses (peace be upon him), and their book is the Torah. In addition they are called the Children of Israel and the Hebrews. All of these, as we have said, are conventional names whose origin is disputed:

"Judaism":

Many interpretations have been offered about the origin of this name, the most important of which are:

1 - Al-Shahrastani, who holds to linguistic derivation, says: "*Hada al-rajul* means the man returned and repented. This name became attached to them because of the words of Moses (peace be upon him): [We have turned back (*hudna*) to You], that is, we have returned and humbly entreated"<sup>(1)</sup>.

In fact, a name derived in this way fits the history of the Jews to a great extent. Whoever leafs through their religious books is astonished at how quickly they rebelled against and disobeyed their prophets, to the point of killing them, then repented, then rebelled, then repented. It is enough that the Torah mentions that they apostatised eight times from their exodus from Egypt under the leadership of Moses (peace be upon him) until their entry into Palestine under Saul, a period of no more than one hundred and fifty years, and many of those times they apostatised were in the lifetime of Moses (peace be upon him) himself.

Thus there is a continuous chain of disobedience and repentance. This is regarded as a psychological phenomenon governing the conduct of the Jews throughout the whole of their religious history, and the Noble Qur'an agrees with their sacred books in bringing this phenomenon to light.

---

(1) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal (Religions and Sects): 1/192, edited by Muhammad Fath Allah Badran, 2nd ed., Maktabat al-Anglo al-Arabiyya, Cairo 1956.

{20}------------------------------------------------

2 - Many historians hold that Judaism is named after "Judah", one of the sons of Jacob (peace be upon him). This opinion is supported by the fact that the tribe of Judah had great standing in the history of the Jews, and it was this tribe that founded the strongest state they had in Palestine.

3 - Another group holds that it is a late name, given to them by the Persians when they occupied Palestine in about the sixth century BC; they called their creed Judaism to distinguish them from other peoples. From that time the word "Jews" came to mean whoever embraced Judaism, even if he was not of the Children of Israel<sup>(2)</sup>.

### "The Children of Israel"

This is the title that the Qur'an, and the Torah too, often gave to the Jews. It is also a title in which the Israelites take great pride, because it is the title by which they affirm or display their descent from Abraham (peace be upon him). Lineage is among the matters to which the Israelites attach the utmost importance and which they are keen to display; by it they mean to affirm the authenticity of their descent and the antiquity of their roots, with the aim of entrenching the idea that they are God's chosen people. For this reason we find their religious books fond of recounting their genealogies in detail and never tiring of repeating them. The Torah relates that Abraham (peace be upon him) took a wife named "Sarah", who bore him Isaac (peace be upon him), and a bondwoman named "Hagar" the Egyptian, who bore him Ishmael (peace be upon him). Isaac (peace be upon him) married a woman named "Rebekah", who bore him twins: the first of them was thick with hair, so they named him Esau, and he became his father's firstborn; after him the second came out with his hand grasping Esau's heel, so they called him Jacob. He is the one named Israel, and hence his sons were called the Children of Israel.

With him the real history of the Children of Israel begins, and it is strange that this history should begin with cunning and deceit. In recounting this beginning the Torah seems to want to establish the principle "the end justifies the means" as a moral value. Here is what the Torah says, so that you may see this for yourselves.

The Torah says, in "the Book of Genesis" [27:1-36]:

> "And it came to pass, when Isaac was old and his eyes were too dim to see, that he called Esau his elder son and said to him, My son. And he said to him, Here I am. And he said, I have now grown old and I do not know

(2) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Yahudiyya (Comparative Religion: Judaism), p. 88, Ahmad Shalabi, 4th ed., Maktabat Nahdat Misr, Cairo

1974.

{21}------------------------------------------------

> the day of my death. Now therefore take your gear, your quiver and your bow, and go out to the field and hunt game for me, and make me savoury food such as I love, and bring it to me that I may eat, so that my soul may bless you before I die.
>
> And Rebekah was listening when Isaac spoke to Esau his son. And Esau went to the field to hunt game and bring it. But Rebekah spoke to Jacob her son, saying, I heard your father speaking to Esau your brother, saying, Bring me game and make me savoury food, that I may eat and bless you before the Lord before my death. Now therefore, my son, listen to my voice in what I command you. Go to the flock and bring me from there two good kids of the goats, and I will make them savoury food for your father such as he loves, and you shall bring it to your father that he may eat, so that he may bless you before his death. And Jacob said to Rebekah his mother, Behold, Esau my brother is a hairy man and I am a smooth man. Perhaps my father will feel me, and I shall seem to him a mocker, and I shall bring a curse upon myself and not a blessing. And his mother said to him, Upon me be your curse, my son; only listen to my voice, and go, bring them to me. So he went and took them and brought them to his mother, and his mother made savoury food such as his father loved. And Rebekah took the fine garments of Esau her elder son which were with her in the house and put them on Jacob her younger son, and she put the skins of the kids of the goats on his hands and on the smooth of his neck, and she gave the savoury food and the bread that she had made into the hand of Jacob her son.
>
> And he came to his father and said, My father. And he said, Here I am; who are you, my son? And Jacob said to his father, I am Esau your firstborn; I have done as you told me. Rise, sit and eat of my game, that your soul may bless me. And Isaac said to his son, How is it that you have found it so quickly, my son? And he said, Because the Lord your God made it easy for me. And Isaac said to Jacob, Come near, that I may feel you, my son, whether you are really my son Esau or not. So Jacob came near to Isaac his father, and he felt him and said, The voice is the voice of Jacob, but the hands are the hands of Esau. And he did not recognise him, because his hands were hairy like the hands of Esau his brother; so he blessed him. And he said, Are you really my son Esau? And he said, I am. And he said, Bring it near to me, that I may eat of my son's game, so that my soul may bless you. So he brought it near to him and he ate, and he brought him wine and he drank. And Isaac his father said to him, Come near now and kiss me, my son. So he came near and kissed him, and he smelled the smell of his garments and blessed him and said, See, the smell of my son is as the smell of a field which the Lord has blessed. May God give you of the dew of heaven and of the fatness of the earth, and plenty of grain and wine. Let peoples serve you and tribes bow down to you. Be lord over your brothers, and let your mother's sons bow down to you. Cursed be those who curse you, and blessed be those who bless you.

{22}------------------------------------------------

> And it came to pass, as soon as Isaac had finished blessing Jacob, and Jacob had scarcely gone out from the presence of Isaac his father, that Esau his brother came in from his hunting. He too made savoury food and brought it to his father, and said to his father, Let my father arise and eat of his son's game, that your soul may bless me. And Isaac his father said to him, Who are you? And he said, I am your son, your firstborn, Esau. And Isaac trembled with a very great trembling and said, Who then is he that hunted game and brought it to me, and I ate of all of it before you came, and I blessed him? Yes, and he shall be blessed. When Esau heard the words of his father, he cried out with a great and exceedingly bitter cry and said to his father, Bless me, me also, O my father. And he said, Your brother came with guile and has taken away your blessing. And he said, Is he not rightly named Jacob? For he has supplanted me these two times: he took away my birthright, and behold, now he has taken away my blessing."

The significance of this text is not hidden: it shows the standards that govern the conduct of the Children of Israel, whether in their internal or their external relations, and that have shaped their psychological and moral make-up to this day.

Then Jacob (peace be upon him) married the two daughters of his maternal uncle:

Leah, who bore him Reuben, Simeon, Levi (from whose line came Moses and Aaron, peace be upon them both), Judah, Issachar and Zebulun.

Rachel, who bore him Joseph and Benjamin.

Then he had a bondwoman named Zilpah, who bore him Gad and Asher.

And by another slave-woman named Bilhah he fathered Dan and Naphtali.

Thus Jacob had twelve sons, and they are the heads of the tribes who entered Egypt with their father Jacob (peace be upon him) in about the sixteenth century BC, when Joseph (peace be upon him) brought them there. Their story is told in full in Surat Yusuf, and it does not differ much from what the Torah, in the Book of Genesis, says.

The illustration on the following page shows the family tree of the Children of Israel down to Moses (peace be upon him).

"The Hebrews"

There is general agreement that this name applies to those descended from the offspring of Abraham (peace be upon him), but there is disagreement about its meaning and derivation:

{23}------------------------------------------------

1 - *Ibri* (Hebrew) is derived from the verb *abara*, which in Hebrew, as in Arabic, means to cross a stage of a road or a valley. It was applied to Abraham (peace be upon him) because he crossed the river and the desert fleeing from his people.

2 - Or it refers to Abraham's remote ancestor Eber, son of Shem, son of Noah, as mentioned in the Book of Genesis, chapter 13, and Abraham (peace be upon him) was known by this title, as stated in the same book, chapter 14.

3 - Or it comes from nomadic life, because the Children of Israel were Bedouin who moved and travelled a great deal in search of pasture and grazing for their livestock. The word *ibri* is thus like the word *badawi* (Bedouin), that is, a dweller of the desert and the wilderness, as opposed to *hadari*, a dweller of cities. It was the civilised nations, the Egyptians, the Canaanites and others, who gave them this title to distinguish them from settled people, since the Israelites came to them from the depths of the desert. For this reason the Jews greatly dislike the name and prefer to be called Israelites or Jews.

Whatever the origin of these titles by which they were known, they are all names that confirm the point we intend: that they are conventional, man-made names reserved for the followers of Moses (peace be upon him).

{24}------------------------------------------------

[Family tree]

- Abraham
  - Ishmael
  - Isaac
    - Jacob, who is named Israel
      - Reuben, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, Zebulun, Joseph, Benjamin, Gad, Asher, Dan, Naphtali
        - (from Levi) Kohath
          - Amram
            - Moses, Aaron
    - Esau

{25}------------------------------------------------

Their history there begins with the story of Joseph and his brothers:

> When they said, "Joseph and his brother are dearer to our father than we are, though we are a strong band. Truly our father is in manifest error. Kill Joseph or cast him out to some land, so that your father's face will be yours alone, and after that you will be a righteous people"

So they threw him into the depths of the well, where some travellers picked him up and sold him to the Aziz of Egypt. Joseph (peace be upon him) was then about seventeen years old. Plotting pursued Joseph (peace be upon him) at the hands of the Aziz's wife, who tried to seduce him, but he refused and held himself chaste. His reward was prison, where he remained for thirteen years, until Pharaoh brought him out to interpret his famous dream and put him in charge of the storehouses of the land, when he was not yet past thirty. Joseph (peace be upon him) succeeded remarkably in organising Egypt's provisioning and its economy. After nine years his brothers came to him from the land of Canaan seeking food; Joseph made himself known to them, then brought his father and all his brothers to live near him in the good land of Egypt.

Since the Israelites were herdsmen and keepers of livestock, Pharaoh settled them in the east of the Delta, in the place where the Sharqiyya governorate lies today. This was the place Joseph (peace be upon him) had advised, for he wanted his family to be near their original homeland and isolated from the rest of the population, so as to preserve their religion and their freedom to practise their worship and their particular customs undisturbed.

The entry of Jacob (peace be upon him) and his sons into Egypt was around the sixteenth century BC, in the time of the Sixteenth Dynasty, when Egypt was under the rule of the Hyksos, who had come upon it as invaders from Asia. Their occupation of Egypt lasted about four centuries, from the 14th Dynasty to the 18th or the 19th Dynasty.

{26}------------------------------------------------

The Israelites lived in Egypt for more than two centuries, enjoying security, prosperity and honour out of regard for Joseph (peace be upon him), who had no doubt become a national hero for saving Egypt and the whole region from the danger of drought and famine. Then the national current grew strong, the Egyptians gained in strength, and they expelled the Hyksos, or what are known as the Shepherd Kings, from all of Egypt under the leadership of Ramesses II, founder of the 19th Dynasty, whom historians regard as the Pharaoh of the oppression. For no sooner had he finished with the Hyksos than he turned to liquidating the Israelite community and its privileges, which in the eyes of the nationalists were bound up with the Hyksos. Pharaoh went so far in tormenting them as to order their males killed and their women taken captive. In that period Moses (peace be upon him) was born, whom God saved from Pharaoh's hand; indeed he grew up and was raised in his court, until he fled from Egypt to Midian for fear of being killed in retribution for killing an Egyptian who had attacked an Israelite. In Midian he married the daughter of a righteous man, said by some narrators to be Shu'ayb (peace be upon him), in return for ten years of service. When the term was over he set out with his family, and in Sinai revelation came to him as a messenger to the Children of Israel. Revelation charged him with a specific command: to call the Pharaoh of Egypt, Merneptah, who took the throne of Egypt after Ramesses and whom historians regard as the Pharaoh of the Exodus, to the oneness of God and to allowing the Children of Israel to leave Egypt. But Pharaoh, despite the signs that God displayed at the hands of Moses in confirmation of his claim, conceded neither matter, and persisted all the more in his arrogance and his persecution of the Israelites, until God commanded Moses to lead the Children of Israel out of Egypt by night. Their departure was accomplished by the miracle of the parting of the sea, in which Pharaoh and his soldiers were drowned<sup>(4)</sup>.

Thus the history of the Israelites in Egypt came to an end. Many historians hold that the period they spent in Egypt, from their entry in the time of the 16th Dynasty to their departure in the time of the 19th Dynasty, was 215 years, which means that their exodus was in about the middle of the fourteenth century BC<sup>(5)</sup>.

(4) See the suras "Yusuf", "Ta Ha" and "Al-Qasas", and also the Book of Genesis.
(5) Qisas al-Anbiya' (Stories of the Prophets), p. 202, Abd al-Wahhab al-Najjar, Mu'assasat al-Halabi, Cairo 1966.

{27}------------------------------------------------

## "The Children of Israel" in Sinai:

The most important events that befell them there, drawing on the Noble Qur'an and the Torah, can be summarised as follows:

First: The revelation of the Torah:

The Torah was sent down to Moses, containing the commandments that the Israelites must follow, whether in what concerns God, in their dealings among themselves, or in their relations with others.

Second: Moses' organisation of the Israelite people:

1 - Moses (peace be upon him) appointed priests from the tribe of the Levites to serve the holy Temple. He restricted to them the right to interpret the law and the divine statutes, and forbade them to take part in government or to acquire private property, so that they might devote themselves entirely to the service of the law. In return he exempted them from military service and made the other tribes responsible for supporting them and meeting their living needs. He appointed Aaron (peace be upon him) head of the priests, with the headship to continue in his line.

This choice gave the tribe moral standing, spiritual influence and great respect in the hearts of the Israelite people.

2 - Moses (peace be upon him) raised from the other tribes an army to protect the call and to strive for the land that God had promised them. He made fighting obligatory on males from the age of twenty to the age of sixty. Then he appointed Joshua son of Nun, of the tribe of Joseph (peace be upon him), commander of the army of conquest, with Eleazar son of Aaron as his minister or religious adviser.

3 - He laid down for the Israelites the order they must follow in the land God had promised they would conquer: he commanded them to divide it, according to the number of the tribes, into twelve equal portions to be distributed among the tribes by lot.

{28}------------------------------------------------

## Third: The worship of the calf:

It happened that, after the Israelites felt secure in their escape from Pharaoh, and when Moses had left them to receive the Torah, they found his return slow, and their souls, which had been influenced by the idolatry of the Egyptians, tempted them to make for themselves a god just as those people had gods. So they made from their ornaments a calf, a body that lowed, and devoted themselves to worshipping it. The Qur'an and the Torah agree on this, except that the Qur'an states that the one who made the calf for them was a man called al-Samiri.

God the Exalted says:

> "And what has made you hasten ahead of your people, O Moses?" He said, "They are close upon my track, and I hastened to You, my Lord, that You might be pleased." He said, "We have tried your people after you, and al-Samiri has led them astray." So Moses returned to his people, angry and grieved. He said, "O my people, did your Lord not make you a fair promise? Did the time seem too long to you, or did you wish that wrath from your Lord should descend upon you, and so you broke your promise to me?" They said, "We did not break our promise to you of our own will, but we were made to carry loads of the people's ornaments, and we cast them in, and so too did al-Samiri throw." Then he brought out for them a calf, a body that lowed, and they said, "This is your god and the god of Moses, but he forgot." Did they not see that it returned them no word and had no power to harm or benefit them? And Aaron had already said to them before, "O my people,

{29}------------------------------------------------

> you are only being tried by it. Truly your Lord is the Most Merciful, so follow me and obey my command." They said, "We shall not cease to devote ourselves to it until Moses returns to us." He said, "O Aaron, what prevented you, when you saw them going astray, from following me? Did you disobey my command?" He said, "O son of my mother, do not seize me by my beard or by my head. I feared that you would say, 'You have caused division among the Children of Israel and did not heed my word.'" He said, "And what is your business, O Samiri?" He said, "I perceived what they did not perceive, so I took a handful from the track of the messenger and cast it, and thus my soul prompted me." He said, "Then go! It shall be yours in this life to say, 'Do not touch me,' and you have an appointment that you will not fail to keep. Look at your god to which you remained devoted: we shall surely burn it and then scatter it in the sea, utterly. Your god is only God, besides whom there is no god; He encompasses all things in knowledge."

Thus we see that while the Qur'an states that al-Samiri was the one who led them astray and that Aaron (peace be upon him) was the one who guided them aright, we see the Torah stating that the one who made the calf for them was Aaron (peace be upon him). This is its text:

> "And when the people saw that Moses delayed to come down from the mountain, the people gathered around Aaron and said to him, Up, make us gods that shall go before us; for as for this Moses, the man who brought us up out of the land of Egypt, we do not know what has become of him. And Aaron said to them, Take off the rings of gold that are in the ears of your wives and your daughters and bring them to me. So all the people took off the rings of gold that were in their ears and brought them to Aaron. And he took them from their hands and fashioned it

{30}------------------------------------------------

> with a graving tool and made it a molten calf. And they said, These are your gods, O Israel, which brought you up out of the land of Egypt. And when Aaron saw it, he built an altar before it; and Aaron made proclamation and said, Tomorrow is a feast to the Lord. And they rose early on the morrow and offered burnt offerings and brought peace offerings; and the people sat down to eat and drink and rose up to play. And the Lord said to Moses, Go, get down, for your people whom you brought up out of the land of Egypt have corrupted themselves. They have turned aside quickly from the way I commanded them. They have made for themselves a molten calf and have worshipped it and sacrificed to it and said, These are your gods, O Israel, which brought you up out of the land of Egypt.
>
> And Moses turned and went down from the mountain with the two tablets of the testimony in his hand, tablets written on both their sides; on the one side and on the other they were written. And the tablets were the work of God, and the writing was the writing of God, engraved upon the tablets .. And it came to pass, as soon as he came near the camp, that he saw the calf and the dancing. And Moses' anger burned hot, and he threw the tablets out of his hands and broke them at the foot of the mountain. Then he took the calf which they had made and burned it with fire and ground it until it was fine powder, and scattered it on the face of the water and made the Children of Israel drink it.
>
> And Moses said to Aaron, What did this people do to you, that you have brought so great a sin upon them? And Aaron said, Let not the anger of my lord burn hot. You know the people, that they are set on evil. They said to me, Make us gods that shall go before us; for as for this Moses, the man who brought us up out of the land of Egypt, we do not know what has become of him. And I said to them, Whoever has gold, let him take it off and give it to me; and I threw it into the fire, and out came this calf." (7)

Fourth: The wandering:

Moses set out with the Children of Israel for the land that God had promised them. But the Israelites, in whose souls humiliation, abasement and servility had taken hold because of what they had suffered at the hands of the Egyptians, no sooner saw the enemy than their hearts flew in panic and terror, and they turned back on their heels in flight, heeding nothing, paying no attention to Moses' call or to his reminding them of the covenant they had made with God. Moses complained to God of their disobedience and rebellion and of his own helplessness, and God decreed for the disobedient that they be denied entry into it and wander in the desert for forty years. So they remained roaming aimlessly in the wilderness until the desert swallowed them up. God did not will that Moses or Aaron (peace be upon them both), or the generation that had forsaken them, should enter Palestine. Aaron died and was buried on a mountain

(7) The Book of Exodus [32:1-24].

{31}------------------------------------------------

called (Hor), one of the mountains of Sinai, and Moses (peace be upon him) followed him; he died sitting on a mountain called (Nebo), looking out over the promised land, and was buried there in a red sandhill, and the traces of his grave were hidden (8).

Here is what the Noble Qur'an says about this event, which in its essence does not differ from what the Torah relates:

> And when Moses said to his people, "O my people, remember God's favour to you when He placed prophets among you and made you kings, and gave you what He had not given to anyone among the worlds. O my people, enter the Holy Land which God has ordained for you, and do not turn back on your heels, or you will turn back as losers." They said, "O Moses, there is a people of tyrants in it, and we shall not enter it until they leave it. If they leave it, then we shall enter." Two men among those who feared God, upon whom God had bestowed His favour, said, "Enter upon them through the gate; when you have entered it you will be victorious. And put your trust in God, if you are believers." They said, "O Moses, we shall never enter it as long as they remain in it. So go, you and your Lord, and fight; we shall sit right here." He said, "My Lord, I have power over none but myself and my brother, so separate us from the defiantly disobedient people." He said, "Then it is forbidden to them for forty years; they shall wander in the land. So do not grieve over the defiantly disobedient people." (9)

(8) See: the Book of Numbers 13, 14, and the Book of Deuteronomy 1, 32, 34.

(9) Al-Ma'ida 5:20-26.

{32}------------------------------------------------

## The Israelites in Palestine

Their history in Palestine, drawn from their own sources and compared with what the Qur'an has said on the matter, can be divided into three periods, beginning with the age of the Judges, then the age of the Kings, and finally the era of division and dispersion.

### First: The age of the Judges:

After the death of Moses (peace be upon him), Joshua son of Nun took charge of the affairs of the Children of Israel, assisted by Eleazar son of Aaron, as Moses had commanded. He entered Palestine with them by force and succeeded in taking Jericho and the areas around it, after annihilating its inhabitants. Then he divided the land he had taken among the tribes as Moses (peace be upon him) had commanded. Joshua continued to look after the affairs of the people, who gave him obedience and loyalty all his life. After his death the people appointed no one to succeed him; each tribe took charge of its own affairs independently and chose from among its elders a chief (*naqib*), to whom it entrusted the command of the army and the appointment of judges to settle disputes among the members of the tribe according to the law and to oversee its application. Thus began what is known in Jewish history as the age of the Judges, which lasted about three centuries and whose features can be summarised as follows:

1 - Each tribe had its own autonomy in matters of land, defence and justice, and was headed by a chief who commanded its army and appointed its judges. The chief was regarded as the chief judge of his tribe. The tribes were therefore closer to allied statelets than to a unified state.

2 - The law was the authority to which all owed obedience and the only manifestation of the unity of the tribes. The chiefs or judges had the task of watching over the people's adherence to the application of the law. If a chief or one of the tribes overstepped the bounds of the law, the other tribes treated it as an enemy that must be fought for breaking the covenant.

3 - Frequent conflicts among the tribes and internal instability. Perhaps the autonomy of the tribes on the one hand, and their solidarity in upholding and carrying out the law on the other, explain the many conflicts and civil wars that are the outstanding feature of the age of the Judges. They also explain how some tribes would at times fight

{33}------------------------------------------------

external enemies on their own, without the other tribes, and their defeat or victory did not mean the defeat or victory of the other tribes.

4 - In some periods, especially when the tribes were fighting a common enemy, a supreme council for war and justice was formed from the chiefs, with supreme authority over all the Israelites. When this council disagreed, or when it faced a problem it could not resolve, it would turn to the priest, the chief interpreter of the law, to make known God's judgement in the matter, for he alone could address God and receive His answers to the questions the chiefs put forward. But these answers that the priest brought did not become binding unless the chiefs approved them; only then did they become binding on all the judges, and whoever contravened them was killed at once. This means that the priest's interpretation, or the answers he received from God, did not become a divine law that had to be carried out unless the chiefs approved it. If the chiefs did not accept them, they became binding on no one and of no value. This means in effect that the real lawgivers were the priests or the rabbis.

A judge or chief had the right to turn directly to the priest in matters concerning his own tribe that he could not resolve, since the high priest was the interpreter of the divine laws and the religious adviser of all the tribes.

In fact, according to what is stated in the Torah, it was Moses (peace be upon him) who laid down the foundations of this system for the Children of Israel<sup>(10)</sup>. All this applied when there was no prophet. If a prophet was sent among them, and he might have been a judge before his mission, he became the sole authority on the law and its interpretation.

But he was not recognised as a prophet unless the chiefs believed him; otherwise he was killed at once. This explains for us the phenomenon of the Israelites killing many of their prophets, as the Qur'an has spoken of in the words of the Exalted:

> And humiliation and wretchedness were stamped upon them, and they incurred wrath from God. That was because they used to disbelieve in the signs of God and kill the prophets without right. That was because they disobeyed and used to transgress. (11)

(11) Al-Baqara 2:61.

(10) See the Book of Exodus 18.

{34}------------------------------------------------

As the Book of Judges relates, they were much given to rebellion against their judges and their prophets.

This age was known for unbelief, heresy and godlessness. Take, for example, what comes in the Book of Judges, chapter 2:

> "And the Children of Israel did evil in the sight of the Lord and served the Baals, and they forsook the God of their fathers who had brought them out of the land of Egypt, and went after other gods, from the gods of the peoples round about them, and bowed down to them and provoked the Lord to anger. And to their judges too they would not listen, but went whoring after other gods."

Such remained the state of the Children of Israel throughout the age of the Judges, until corruption reached the judges themselves, who exploited their offices for gain and bribery, so that their judgements followed their whims and their personal interests. Their standing in the eyes of the people was lost, and corruption and godlessness grew among the Israelites until the law, or the system of the judges, was no longer fit to unite the people. Then it happened that the Israelites were defeated before the Philistines, who seized the holy Ark; for the Israelites used to carry it with them into the great battles, to seek victory through it and to strengthen themselves in the fighting.

At that point the people asked a prophet of theirs named Samuel to choose for them a king who would rule, establish order among them and unite their ranks, as other peoples were ruled. Let us read what the First Book of Samuel says about this period:

> "And it came to pass, when Samuel was old, that he made his sons judges over Israel. But his two sons did not walk in his ways; they turned aside after gain, took bribes and perverted judgement. Then the elders of Israel gathered together and came to Samuel ... and said to him: Behold, you have grown old, and your sons do not walk in your ways. Now make us a king to judge us like all the nations. But the thing displeased Samuel, when they said, Give us a king to judge us. And Samuel prayed to the Lord, and the Lord said: Hearken to the voice of the people in all that they say to you, for they have not rejected you, but they have rejected me, that I should not reign over them, according to all the works that they have done since the day I brought them up out of the land of Egypt to this day, forsaking me and serving other gods." (12)

Thus the second age began after the collapse of the age of the Judges, and the Qur'an speaks of

(12) Chapter 8:1-9. See also chapters 4-6, which speak of the Philistines' seizure of the holy Ark; this is a significant indication that the Torah was lost at an early stage in the history of the Children of Israel.

{35}------------------------------------------------

> the chiefs of the Children of Israel after Moses, when they said to a prophet of theirs: "Raise up for us a king, and we will fight in the way of God." He said: "Might it be that, if fighting were prescribed for you, you would not fight?" They said: "And why should we not fight in the way of God, when we have been driven out of our homes and our children?" But when fighting was prescribed for them, they turned away, all but a few of them; and God knows well the wrongdoers. (246) And their prophet said to them: "God has raised up Saul (Talut) as king over you." They said: "How can he have kingship over us, when we are more entitled to kingship than he, and he has not been given abundance of wealth?" He said: "God has chosen him over you and has increased him abundantly in knowledge and in stature; and God gives His kingship to whom He wills; and God is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing." (247) And their prophet said to them: "The sign of his kingship is that the Ark will come to you, in which is tranquillity from your Lord and a remnant of what the family of Moses and the family of Aaron left behind, borne by the angels. Surely in that is a sign for you, if you are believers." (248) And when Saul set out with the troops, he said: "God will test you with a river. Whoever drinks from it is not of me, and whoever does not taste it is of me, except one who scoops up a scoopful with his hand." But they drank from it, all but a few of them. And when he and those who believed with him had crossed it, they said: "We have no strength today against Goliath (Jalut) and his troops." Those who were certain that they would meet God said: "How many a small band has overcome a large band by the leave of

{36}------------------------------------------------

> God! And God is with the steadfast." (249) And when they went out to face Goliath and his troops, they said: "Our Lord, pour out steadfastness upon us, make our feet firm, and grant us victory over the unbelieving people." (250) So they routed them by the leave of God, and David killed Goliath, and God gave him kingship and wisdom and taught him of what He willed. And were it not for God's repelling some people by means of others, the earth would have been corrupted; but God is bountiful to all the worlds. (251) (13)

## Second: The Age of the Kings

### 1. Saul, whom the Qur'an calls "Talut"

Samuel chose him as king over the Children of Israel at the direction of the Lord. When the people were required to pledge allegiance to him, their reply was as the Noble Qur'an relates:

> They said: "How can he have kingship over us, when we are more entitled to kingship than he, and he has not been given abundance of wealth?" He said: "God has chosen him over you and has increased him abundantly in knowledge and in stature; and God gives His kingship to whom He wills; and God is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing." (14)

So the people answered him, though many of them did so unwillingly. Saul led them in successful battles until victory over their enemy was won for him at the hands of David (peace be upon him), who decided the battles by killing their commander Goliath. The Qur'an agrees with the Old Testament on the substance of this story, except that the books of the Jews go into many details about the bloody struggle between Saul and David after the victory over the Philistines, and then about the continuing struggle between David and the sons of Saul, until

(13) Al-Baqara 2:246-251.

(14) Al-Baqara 2:247.

{37}------------------------------------------------

the matter was settled for David by the slaughter of Saul's sons and their commanders, and he was installed as king over all the Children of Israel.<sup>(15)</sup> In this the language of the Qur'an parts company with that of the Torah in speaking of David (peace be upon him): the Qur'an speaks of him as a prophet possessing all the nobility and fine character that a prophet possesses, whereas the Torah speaks of him as a king possessing all the harshness and violence of a tyrannical king. This has a profound significance for distinguishing the language of revelation from the language of human beings.

### 2. David (peace be upon him)

Thus David became the second king over the Children of Israel. Once his rule was secure and the people had submitted to him in obedience and loyalty, he turned to subjugating the neighbouring kingdoms in Palestine, and succeeded in taking the most important of its cities, Jerusalem, which became the capital of Israel in his reign and in that of Solomon (peace be upon them both).<sup>(16)</sup>

### 3. Solomon (peace be upon him)

According to the First Book of Kings, he began his reign by killing his elder brother and the commander of the army. After that his kingship and sovereignty were secure, and the people submitted to him in obedience and loyalty. Solomon managed the affairs of the state with a skill and wisdom that enabled him to establish himself firmly throughout all the land that David had conquered, and he added to it. In his reign the state enjoyed stability, prosperity and the respect of the neighbouring states, so much so that the Pharaoh of Egypt sought his favour through marriage. This is a sign of Solomon's strength, for the Pharaoh of Egypt could not have given his daughter in marriage to Solomon unless Solomon's kingdom had reached such strength as to make him the equal of a great king like Pharaoh.

This enabled Solomon to accomplish the most important event in the history of the Israelites after Moses (peace be upon him): the building of the holy Temple in Jerusalem. "And it was in the four hundred and eightieth year after the Children of Israel came out of the land of Egypt, in the fourth year of the reign of Solomon (peace be upon him)."<sup>(17)</sup> The holy Ark was moved into it, and "there was nothing in the Ark except the two tablets of stone that Moses had placed there at Horeb, when the Lord made a covenant with the Children of Israel as they came out of the land of Egypt."<sup>(18)</sup>

(15) See First and Second Samuel.

(16) See the Second Book of Samuel.

(17) First Book of Kings 6:1.

(18) First Book of Kings 8:9.

{38}------------------------------------------------

As is the way with the Children of Israel, and according to their own books, when Solomon grew old and his grip on their necks loosened, the people rebelled against him and looked for a new king. His son Jeroboam conspired against him together with many of the elders of the Children of Israel, but fled from his father to Egypt when his plot was discovered. Many of the kingdoms that had been in Solomon's hand also rebelled against him.

Thus Solomon died, as the First Book of Kings says, with his kingdom shrunk to less than what he had inherited from his father David (peace be upon him).

In fact the rebelliousness of the Israelites is a prominent feature of their whole history as their own books present it. We find that their relationship with their rulers, whether prophets, judges or kings, rests on hostility and watchful waiting, as if it were a relationship between two opposing sides in which the stronger prevails. That is to say, there is no prophet, judge or king who does not come in the wake of disobedience and rebellion and whose career does not end in disobedience and rebellion.

A ruler's hold over them is secure only in proportion to the violence and force he possesses, and the measure of their obedience and loyalty to him matches the measure of his might. But at the first sign of weakness or tolerance, or when his grip on their necks loosens, the people rebel against him and hold him in contempt, and he ends either killed or cast into the corners of oblivion and neglect. Such remained their condition until their state ended in disintegration and decline.

### Third: The Era of Division

After the death of Solomon (peace be upon him), around 935 BC, the people gathered in Jerusalem to install Rehoboam in his father's place. But they made it a condition that he lift from their shoulders much of what Solomon had imposed on them in the way of decrees. He refused, and most of the people sided with his brother Jeroboam, who had returned from Egypt after their father's death; only the tribe of Judah remained with Rehoboam.

In this way the state was divided between the two sons of Solomon (peace be upon him) into two rival states.

One was in the north and was called Israel; its capital was Shechem, which Jeroboam built to be the capital of his kingdom.

The other was in the south and was called Judah; its capital was Jerusalem and its king was Rehoboam.

{39}------------------------------------------------

The fighting between the two states, in which the blood of the Israelites flowed in rivers, never stopped, as the First Book of Kings says: "And there was war between Rehoboam and Jeroboam all their days." In the end it brought the Israelites to dispersion and wandering across distant lands.

The two brothers agreed on nothing, except that they were at one in altering the law, in godlessness and in the worship of idols.

As for Jeroboam, the First Book of Kings [12:25-28] says:

> "And Jeroboam built Shechem ... and Jeroboam said in his heart ... If this people go up to offer sacrifices in the house of the Lord at Jerusalem, the heart of this people will turn back to Rehoboam king of Judah, and they will kill me ... So the king took counsel and made two calves of gold, and said to them: It is too much for you to go up to Jerusalem; behold your gods, O Israel, who brought you up out of the land of Egypt."

Rehoboam was in no better state than his brother. The First Book of Kings [14:21-24] says of him:

> "And Rehoboam the son of Solomon reigned in Judah. Rehoboam was forty-one years old when he began to reign, and he reigned seventeen years in Jerusalem, the city that the Lord had chosen out of all the tribes of the Children of Israel to put His name there ... And Judah did evil in the sight of the Lord, and they provoked Him to jealousy more than all that their fathers had done, with the sins that they committed. For they also built for themselves high places, pillars and sacred poles on every high hill and under every green tree. And there were also sodomites in the land; they did according to all the abominations of the nations whom the Lord had driven out before the Children of Israel."

It is worth noting here that, in addition to that corruption, and perhaps as a result of it, the first destruction of the Temple that Solomon had built, and in which he had placed the holy Ark containing the Torah, took place in the reign of Rehoboam at the hands of the Egyptians, perhaps at Jeroboam's instigation. This counts as the second time the Torah was lost. The First Book of Kings [14:25] says:

> "And in the fifth year of King Rehoboam, Shishak king of Egypt came up against Jerusalem, and he took the treasures of the house of the Lord and the treasures of the king's house; he took everything ..."

{40}------------------------------------------------

With the division of the state and its religious decay, the history of the Children of Israel became an unbroken chain of battles and defeats, both among themselves and with their neighbours, until God put an end to the Children of Israel as a people and as a state at the hands of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, who destroyed them utterly and took those of them who remained as his slaves. This is what is known in Jewish history as the Babylonian captivity, around 586 BC. In it took place the second destruction of the Temple and of all Jerusalem. The Second Book of Kings, chapter 25, describes what Nebuchadnezzar did to the Israelites very well, and it is enough for us to quote this passage from it:

> "And in the fifth month, which was the nineteenth year of King Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon, Nebuzaradan, the chief of the guard, a servant of the king of Babylon, came to Jerusalem. And he burned the house of the Lord and the king's house and all the houses of Jerusalem; every great man's house he burned with fire. And all the walls of Jerusalem round about were broken down by all the army of the Chaldeans who were with the chief of the guard. And the rest of the people who were left in the city, and the deserters who had gone over to the king of Babylon, and the rest of the multitude, Nebuzaradan the chief of the guard carried away captive."

But around 538 BC Cyrus, king of Persia, occupied the land of Babylon, released the Israelites from captivity and allowed those who wished to return to Palestine. They returned not as a state but as a community with the right to practise its religious and social rites freely under the authority of Persian rule, to which Palestine was then subject. It is said that in this period the Persians gave the Israelites the name "Jews" (*Yahud*) and their creed the name "Judaism", to distinguish them from the other inhabitants of Palestine. From that date the position of the Jews in Palestine remained unsettled, as Persian, Syrian and Egyptian occupation followed one after another, until Alexander of Macedon took it in 320 BC and after him it passed to the Ptolemies. Thereafter Palestine remained under the rule of the Romans most of the time until the mission of Jesus (peace be upon him).

{41}------------------------------------------------

## Their Sacred Books

### First: The Torah

The Torah is the sacred book of the Jews.
Its meaning is the law, or the religious teachings, that Moses conveyed to his people at Sinai after they came out of Egypt. In its present form it consists of five books:
**The Book of Genesis, or Creation:**

It contains the story of the creation of the world and of man, the story of Adam's sin and his expulsion from the Garden of Eden, which in their view was on earth and not in heaven, and his life after leaving the Garden, and his descendants down to the event of the Flood.

Then the rise of the peoples after it, then the story of Abraham, his journeys and his offspring, Isaac and Jacob and his twelve sons, and Ishmael (peace be upon him).

Then the story of Joseph, what befell him at the hands of his brothers, and his entry into Egypt until he attained great standing there.

Then the entry of Jacob and his sons, the brothers of Joseph, into Egypt. This book ends with the death of Joseph (peace be upon him).

### The Book of Exodus

It deals with the story of the Children of Israel in Egypt and their persecution.
Then the appearance of Moses and his prophethood, his dialogue with Pharaoh, and the signs with which Pharaoh and his people were afflicted, up to Moses' leading the Children of Israel out of Egypt, what happened to them and what they did in the desert, and the sending down of the Torah to Moses.

Then the book continues to narrate their history in this period until Moses brings them to the gates of Palestine.

{42}------------------------------------------------

## The Book of Leviticus, or of the Priests

This book contains a great deal of legislation, commandments, rulings, rites, acts of worship, offerings and other legal matters.

### The Book of Numbers

It is so called because it is full of the counting and division of the tribes of the Children of Israel, the ordering of their encampments by tribe and the census of their males, in addition to the story of the Children of Israel in Sinai. In this it is a continuation of the Book of Exodus.

### The Book of Deuteronomy

Its meaning is repetition and restatement. It sets out the Ten Commandments, and it goes over again what the Book of Leviticus discussed concerning the permitted and the forbidden, the rulings and the law.

This book also speaks of the choice of Joshua son of Nun as successor to Moses, and it ends by speaking of the death of Moses (peace and blessings be upon him) and his burial in the mountains of Moab.

### Second: Other Books

They number thirty-four books, written in the names of their prophets and rabbis after Moses.

They are a historical narrative of the conditions of the Jews and of their prophets and events after their entry into Palestine until their state there came to an end. Together with the Torah they make thirty-nine books, which are given the name now known as the Old Testament. These books are:

Joshua - Judges - Ruth - First Samuel - Second Samuel - First Kings - Second Kings - First Chronicles - Second Chronicles - Ezra - Nehemiah - Esther - Job - Psalms - Proverbs - Ecclesiastes - Song of Songs - Isaiah - Jeremiah - Lamentations of Jeremiah - Ezekiel - Daniel - Hosea - Joel - Amos - Obadiah - Jonah - Micah - Nahum - Habakkuk - Zephaniah - Haggai - Zechariah - Malachi.

{43}------------------------------------------------

## Third: The Talmud

It is a sacred book for the Jews, like the Torah, composed by the rabbis and learned men of the Jews as an interpretation of the Torah. Its meaning is the book of instruction in the religion of the Jews and its ethics.

It was written down more than once. The first to write it was a rabbi named "Yohanas", around 150 CE, who called it the "Mishnah", that is, the repeated law, as an interpretation and completion of the Torah.

Then the rabbis of the Jews made many additions to it in the following years, until it was completed at the hands of a rabbi named "Judah" in 216 CE, after he had gathered the original text, added the commentaries and additions of the rabbis, and then issued it as a single book.

But the "Mishnah" was very obscure for ordinary Jews, so the scholars and rabbis of the Jews set about writing commentaries and glosses on it, and these additions were called the "Gemara".

The Talmud is made up of the "Gemara" and the "Mishnah".<sup>(19)</sup>

---

(19) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Yahudiyya (Comparative Religion: Judaism), p. 271 ff.

{44}------------------------------------------------

## Their Beliefs (20)

The beliefs of the Jews revolve around the following matters:

**First: The impermissibility of abrogation (*naskh*)**

They claim that there can be only one law, which began with Moses and was completed by him; no law came down from heaven before him, and no law will come down after him.

On this basis abrogation is impermissible in their view. They argue that abrogation means turning away from an earlier command to a command that has appeared to God to be better than it; and since such a change of mind (*bada'*) is impossible for God, abrogation is impossible.

They hold fast to this ruling in order to take it as proof that the law of Moses is the only divine law, before and after which no other law has been sent down. Yet this belief is refuted by explicit texts in the Torah and their other books which lifted many rulings that had previously been commanded, or permitted or forbidden things contrary to what had previously been commanded. Ibn Hazm cited numerous examples of these. (21)

**Second: Anthropomorphism (*tashbih*)**

They hold that God has a body, and they take literally everything in the verses of the Torah such as "the image", "speaking face to face", "speaking aloud", the descent upon Mount Sinai and other such texts. They believe that God has an image, a shape, a body and attributes resembling those of man, except that they distinguish between God, exalted be He above what they say, and man by the enormity of His size, which they imagine so vast and huge as far to exceed that of man. Yet He is liable, in their view, to what man is liable to: forgetfulness, grief, regret, weariness, the need for rest and other such accidents that befall man. The texts of the Torah and their other books fashion of God a human image that is explicit in its corporealism

(20) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal (Religions and Sects), 1/192 ff.

(21) Al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa' wa al-Nihal (The Decisive Word on Religions, Heresies and Sects), vol. 1, pp. 103-105, Maktabat al-Muthanna, Baghdad.

{45}------------------------------------------------

and anthropomorphism. Space does not permit listing the texts on this subject, so many and widespread are they; among them, for example:

> "And God said: Let us make man in our image, after our likeness ... So God created man in His own image; in the image of God He created him." [Genesis 1:26-27]

> "Whoever sheds the blood of man, by man shall his blood be shed, for God made man in His own image." [Genesis 9:6]

> "In the day that God created man, in the likeness of God He made him." [Genesis 5:1]

> "And on the seventh day God finished His work that He had done, and He rested on the seventh day from all His work that He had done. And He blessed the seventh day and sanctified it, because in it He rested from all His work that God had created and made." [Genesis 2:2-3]

> "And the Lord saw that the wickedness of man was great on the earth ... And the Lord was grieved that He had made man on the earth, and He was sorry in His heart." [Genesis 6:5-6]

The Talmud, which is their sacred book after the Torah and is regarded as a commentary, exposition and interpretation of the Torah, confirms this anthropomorphism and corporealism, saying: (The breadth of their Creator's forehead, from its top to His nose, is five thousand cubits; on His head is a crown containing a thousand qintars of gold, and on His finger a ring from which the sun and the stars shine.) See Al-Fasl, vol. 1, p. 221.

**Third: Predestination and free will**

On this they hold two doctrines.

One group affirms the freedom of the human will and its independence of the divine will: man creates his own acts, good and evil, and is responsible for them, and God the Exalted neither wills evil nor does it. The doctrine of this group is close to the position of the Mu'tazila in Islam.

These are called the "Rationalists" or the "Rabbanites", and they are the class of thinkers among the Jews.

The other group, the majority of the Jews, holds to predestination: all action is in God's hand, and there is no turning back His decree, His destiny or His will. Man has neither will nor choice before the Lord, in whose hand is everything; man's life and death, his misery and happiness, his good and his evil, are all the work of God.

{46}------------------------------------------------

This group is called the "Karaites" or the "Textualists".
They have a counterpart among the schools of the theologians in Islam, such as the Jabriyya, who say that man is like a feather hanging in the air, swayed by the wind wherever it blows.

### Fourth: The Return (*raj'a*)

This means the belief that a person will return after his death or his disappearance. The Jews believe in it, and they rely in this on two things:

The first: the story of ('Uzayr), whom God caused to die for a hundred years and then raised up.

The second: the story of Aaron (peace be upon him), who died in the wilderness during the lifetime of Moses. They have attributed to Moses, falsely and slanderously, that he killed him with the tablets out of envy and jealousy, because Aaron was dearer to the Jews than he was. Indeed the Jews, in their accounts of Aaron, revere him and incline to him more than to Moses (peace be upon him).

They differed over the manner of his death.

Some of them said that he truly died and will live again and return to them.
Others said that he only disappeared and will return.

The roots of this belief may go back to the early beginnings of Jewish thought, for we find the Torah saying of "Enoch", the fifth descendant of Adam (peace be upon him), that his life did not end in death like that of his fathers and brothers, but that he disappeared or was taken up. It says:

> "And Enoch lived sixty-five years and begot Methuselah. And Enoch walked, after he begot Methuselah, three hundred years, and begot sons and daughters. So all the days of Enoch were three hundred and sixty-five years. And Enoch walked with God, and he was not, for God took him." [Genesis 5:21-24]

We find a claim of return like this among many sects of the Shia. The first to profess it and spread it among the Muslims was Abdullah ibn Saba', who had been a Jew and became a Muslim in the time of Uthman ibn Affan (may God be pleased with him).

### Fifth: The awaited Messiah

Al-Shahrastani says that the Jews are agreed: (that in the Torah there is good tidings of one who will come after Moses; they differ only in identifying that one, or in adding to that one.

{47}------------------------------------------------

The coming forth of one at the end of time, who is the shining star by whose light the earth will be illumined, is likewise agreed upon; the Jews await him, and the Sabbath is that man's day.) (22)

---

(22) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal 1/200.

{48}------------------------------------------------

## The Sects of the Jews

The Jews split into more than seventy sects, the most important of which are:

**First: The Pharisees**

The name means the separated or the dissenters. They call themselves (the rabbis) or (the Rabbanites).

**Their beliefs:**

1. They believe that the Torah was created from eternity on holy tablets and that God then revealed it to Moses.

2. They believe in the Last Day, the resurrection, the angels and the other matters known only through revelation.

3. They incline to asceticism and call for monastic withdrawal, because the world is an abode of trial, and the believer must guard against its evil and keep away from its burdens and its passing pleasures, seeking salvation and eternal life in the bliss of the hereafter. They forbid the offering of sacrifices in the temples and the other rites that the priests invented.

4. They believe in the infallibility of the rabbis, and hold that whatever issues from them is revelation from God flowing on their tongues. It was they who composed the Talmud, by inspiration from God, as an interpretation and explanation of the Torah; hence the Talmud is as sacred as the Torah, indeed more sacred, because it is the key to understanding the Torah and knowing its purposes.

Among their sayings on the sanctity of their rabbis: The believer must regard the sayings of the rabbis as the law, for their sayings are the word of the living God; if the rabbi says that your right hand is the left, and the reverse, believe what he says and do not dispute with him.

5. They hold to predestination, and that everything has been decreed in a way that cannot be changed.

6. They do not acknowledge Jesus and do not believe in his mission. They hold that the Messiah has not yet come, and they stand waiting for him to lead them to salvation from sins and to the dominion of the Children of Israel over the world.

**Second: The Sadducees**

They are named after a man called "Zadok", who was the high priest in the time of Solomon

{49}------------------------------------------------

(peace be upon him). Some historians hold that calling them Sadducees, that is, "the truthful", is a case of naming by opposites, since they were known for denial and for giving the lie to all Jews other than themselves.

### Their beliefs:

1. They deny the resurrection and the next life, and that there is a reckoning and a recompense of Paradise and Hell; life is only the life of this world, and recompense is only recompense in this world. They also deny that God has angels, or that there are jinn or devils.
2. They deny that the Torah that exists now is the one that Moses brought; it is rather sayings collected from the lips of the priests and the elders of the Jews. For this reason they do not believe in its sanctity, just as they do not believe in the Talmud.
3. They deny divine decree and destiny, and hold that no one has any authority over man and his freedom: his acts are the work of his own hand and not the work of anyone else, so God has nothing to do with our acts or our states.
4. They deny the awaited Messiah and do not believe that he will come.
5. They hold to the corporeality and anthropomorphism of God, glory be to Him and exalted be He; in their belief He is in the image of man. And they ascribe a son to Him, saying that Ezra ('Uzayr) is the son of God, exalted be God high above what they say.

### Third: The Karaites

They were given this name because they occupied themselves with writing out the law and reciting it. They are also called the sages, or the preachers.

### Their beliefs:

1. They believe in the Talmud and its sanctity. As for the Torah, they acknowledge part of it and deny the rest, holding that it was composed by the other sects and is not part of the Torah that was sent down to Moses (peace be upon him).
2. They permit independent reasoning (*ijtihad*) and opinion concerning the Torah, and freedom to adopt some of its rulings or not to adopt them; but in the case of the Talmud they do not permit this. (23)

(23) Al-Shahrastani, Al-Milal wa al-Nihal 1/192-200; Shalabi, Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Yahudiyya, p. 98 ff.

{50}------------------------------------------------

## The Character of the Jews

By this we mean the character of the deviant among them. As for those who became Muslims and were good Muslims, and they are, alas, a small group, they are our brothers who preceded us in faith, of whom the Qur'an said:

> And among the people of Moses is a community who guide by the truth and by it act justly. (24)

As for the deviant, it is enough to leaf through the sacred books of the Jews to see how they stood at the opposite extreme from their messengers and the values and virtuous character those messengers represented, and how hard their hearts were, never softening to good, never answering to the truth and never humbled by the remembrance of God, as though they were:

> like stones, or harder still; for there are stones from which rivers gush forth, and there are some that split open so that water comes out of them, and there are some that fall down for fear of God; and God is not unaware of what you do. (25)

But nothing gushed forth from the hearts of these people except black rancour against other believing and good peoples. And God Almighty spoke the truth:

> You will surely find the most intense of people in enmity towards those who believe to be the Jews and those who associate partners with God. (26)

They altered, among the many things they altered, God's favour to them in the messengers and books He sent to guide them to the truth and to acting upon it. Their sick souls altered those favours

(24) Al-A'raf 7:159.

(25) Al-Baqara 2:74.

(26) Al-Ma'ida 5:82.

{51}------------------------------------------------

into a racial privilege, and their devil made it seem fair to them that they are God's chosen people, whom He chose for their own selves and persons. Yet God often reminded them that His honouring of them was only in proportion to their honouring of the truth, their exalting of the Word of God in their souls and their complete submission to God's command and prohibition; otherwise they are like cattle, or even further astray. Let us read these verses, which set out their false claims and then refute them:

> And they said: "The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days." Say: "Have you taken a covenant from God, so that God will not break His covenant? Or do you say about God what you do not know?" (80) Not so! Whoever earns an evil deed and is encompassed by his sin, those are the companions of the Fire; in it they will abide for ever. (81) (27)

> And they said: "None shall enter Paradise except one who is a Jew or a Christian." Those are their wishful fancies. Say: "Bring your proof, if you are truthful." (111) Not so! Whoever submits his face to God while doing good, he shall have his reward with his Lord; no fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve. (112) (28)

> And they said: "Be Jews or Christians and you will be rightly guided." Say: "Rather, the religion of Abraham, the upright in faith, and he was not of those who associate partners with God." (135) Say: "We believe in God and in what has been sent down to us, and what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes, and what was given to Moses and Jesus, and what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction

(27) Al-Baqara 2:80-81.
(28) Al-Baqara 2:111-112.

{52}------------------------------------------------

> between any of them, and to Him we submit." (136) (29)
