> He has ordained for you of religion what He enjoined upon Noah, and what We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses and Jesus: "Establish the religion and do not be divided in it." Grievous to the idolaters is that to which you call them. God chooses for Himself whom He wills, and guides to Himself whoever turns to Him. (Al-Shura 42:13)

{93}------------------------------------------------

- Al-Zukhruf:

> And when Jesus came with the clear proofs, he said: "I have come to you with wisdom, and to make clear to you some of that about which you differ; so fear God and obey me. God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him; this is a straight path." (Al-Zukhruf 43:63-64)

- Al-Hadid:

> And We sent Noah and Abraham, and placed in their descendants prophethood and the Book; among them are some who are guided, but many of them are transgressors. Then We sent Our messengers following in their footsteps, and We sent Jesus son of Mary after them and gave him the Gospel, and We placed in the hearts of those who followed him compassion and mercy. And monasticism they invented; We did not prescribe it for them, except in seeking God's good pleasure, yet they did not observe it as it should be observed. So We gave those of them who believed their reward, but many of them are transgressors. (Al-Hadid 57:26-27)

- Al-Saff:

> And when Jesus son of Mary said: "O Children of Israel, I am the messenger of God to you, confirming the Torah that was before me, and bringing glad tidings of a messenger who will come after me, whose name is Ahmad." But when he came to them with the clear proofs, they said: "This is plain sorcery." (Al-Saff 61:6)

{94}------------------------------------------------

> O you who believe, be God's helpers, as Jesus son of Mary said to the disciples: "Who will be my helpers in the cause of God?" The disciples said: "We are God's helpers." So a party of the Children of Israel believed and a party disbelieved; and We strengthened those who believed against their enemy, and they became the victors. (Al-Saff 61:14)

These are all the verses that draw for us a complete picture of Jesus (peace be upon him). From the plain sense of these verses, and keeping away from allegorical interpretation, we can establish the following points:

- Jesus (peace be upon him) is the Messenger of God to the Children of Israel; his book is the Gospel, and he confirms the Torah that was before him.

- His birth without a father and his speaking in the cradle were heralding signs of his prophethood; and the miracles he brought, raising the dead, healing the blind from birth and the leper, and telling of the unseen, were the work of God and by His leave. All of them are signs that support one another and point to a single goal: to show the truth of his claim to be a messenger.

- Jesus, though a messenger with a prominent place among the prophets and messengers, is no more than a human being like all other human beings and a servant of God like all His servants; he has no power over anything from God, for himself or for anyone else. These meanings form a main thread running through all the verses that speak of his life, verses which stress this human lineage of Jesus by calling him, on most of the occasions on which they speak of him, "son of Mary".

- The lineage of Jesus (peace be upon him) goes back to Abraham (peace be upon him) through his mother Mary the Virgin, daughter of Imran, who was known among her people for her purity, chastity, faith, piety and noble descent. Like any pure and chaste young woman, she was frightened by the angel's tidings of a son to be born to her without a father, so much so that she wished for death, had it not been for her faith and her trust that God would not abandon her.

- Jesus (peace be upon him) is like the rest of the messengers and prophets: their religion is one and their message is one in its foundations, namely Islam. The difference between them lies only in some practical rulings, and each of them continues the call of the other and confirms the messengers and books that came before him.

{95}------------------------------------------------

- Jesus (peace be upon him) was the last of the messengers from the Children of Israel, but he gave glad tidings of a messenger to come after him whose name is Ahmad.

- The claim that some human beings are sons of God is an ancient saying that seeped into the beliefs of the Jews from the beliefs of the pagans; their saying "Ezra is the son of God" was taken from these pagan beliefs.

- The Israelites were of three kinds with regard to Jesus (peace be upon him) and his miracles:

**One kind** believed in him as a servant of God and a messenger from Him to them, to whom the Gospel was sent down, confirming the Torah and correcting the Jews' alterations in belief and conduct. At the head of these believers were the disciples. This kind regarded the miracles Jesus brought as proof of his truthfulness and as God's support for him in his claim.

**Another kind** disbelieved in him, called his claim a lie, and regarded his miracles as a form of sorcery, charlatanry and conjuring.

**And another kind** deviated concerning him and his signs and claimed divinity for him: he is a god, or the son of a god, or the third of three; and they took his miracles and his birth as proof of their claim.

Both of the last two kinds, those who called him a liar and those who deviated, built their position on conjecture and desire. The one who called him a liar was inconsistent, for he distinguished between Jesus and the earlier messengers in what a miracle proves. The one who deviated was likewise arbitrary in distinguishing between Jesus and the earlier messengers in what a miracle proves, for he gave Jesus a special status, resting on no evidence, that set him above the earlier messengers who had been supported by miracles of the same kind as those with which Jesus was supported. Turning a staff into a serpent is no less miraculous than raising the dead or healing the sick.

- The disciples asked Jesus (peace be upon him) that God send down to them a table of food from heaven, as a sign by which their faith and their certainty in Jesus and his message would increase. God answered Jesus's prayer for this table and made it a festival for him and his followers.

- Jesus (peace be upon him), as a messenger, made lawful some of what had been forbidden to the Children of Israel; but the verses do not state explicitly whether what he made lawful had been forbidden by God or forbidden by the Jews themselves.

- Jesus was neither killed nor crucified; rather, God raised him up, and his life on earth ended in a way the Qur'an does not disclose.

{96}------------------------------------------------

This is the life of Jesus and his portrait as the Qur'an has drawn it. If, from the other side, we look at the Gospels by the same standard by which we looked at the verses, that is, the direct and plain meaning of the text, without our intervening by commentary or allegorical interpretation, we would find that in substance those texts do not differ from the Qur'an on the questions concerning the humanity of Jesus (peace be upon him) and his mission, except on the question of the crucifixion and the killing; and that there is nothing in them that explicitly indicates the divinity of Jesus, except by a kind of forcing and arbitrariness in the use of certain words, interpreting them suggestively and taking them in one of their possible meanings. Let us take some examples of this from the Gospels:

The Gospel of Matthew: Jesus says to Iblis, when he asked Jesus to bow down to him and he would give him the kingdom and the glory:

> "Go away, Satan, for it is written: You shall bow down to the Lord your God, and Him alone shall you worship." (11)

And he says:

> "Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets. I have not come to abolish but to fulfil." (12)

Then he affirms his prophethood and his mission, saying:

> "Whoever receives you receives me, and whoever receives me receives the One who sent me. Whoever receives a prophet in the name of a prophet will receive a prophet's reward." (13)

> "And when he came to his own country he taught them in their synagogue, so that they were astonished and said: Where did this man get this wisdom and these mighty works? Is this not the carpenter's son? Is not his mother called Mary, and his brothers James, Joseph, Simon and Judas? And are not all his sisters with us? Where then did this man get all this? And they took offence at him. But Jesus said to them: A prophet is not without honour except in his own country and in his own house. And he did not do many mighty works there because of their unbelief." (14)

> "And behold, one came up and said to him: Good Teacher, what good thing must I do to have eternal life? And he said to him: Why do you call me good? No one is good but One, and that is God..." (15)

Indeed, his own people knew him only as a prophet:

> "And when he entered Jerusalem the whole city was stirred, saying: Who is this? And the crowds said: This is Jesus the prophet, from Nazareth

> to them: I am ascending to my Father and your Father, to my God and your God. Mary Magdalene went and told the disciples that she had seen the Lord, and that he had said these things to her." (31)

Reviewing these texts, we can establish that there is no essential disagreement on two matters:

First: the nature of Jesus (peace be upon him).

As to his nature, he is a human being and a messenger, whom God supported with miracles to confirm his claim, like all the messengers. And if the Qur'an most often called him "son of Mary", to stress his human lineage and to point to his birth without a father, as we have said, then the Gospels, in calling him "the Son of Man", affirm, by sound religious logic, the very meaning the Qur'an intended. As for calling him "the Son of God", this is a figurative relationship signifying nurture, obedience and loyalty, and in no way a real relationship. The proof is that Jesus himself, as in the texts we have cited and many others besides, used to address his followers in the name of his Father and their Father who is in heaven. So by this relationship Jesus has no special status, nor any sign that distinguishes his nature from that of other human beings (34).

---

(31) Chapter 20:16-18.

(34) Dr. Charles Guignebert, in his book Al-Masihiyya: Nash'atuha wa Tatawwuruha (Christianity: Its Origin and Development), discusses this question at length and concludes that: "The firm result of the scholars' studies is that Jesus never claimed to be the awaited Messiah, and never called himself the Son of God... Nor does any text of the Gospels allow us to apply the expression 'Son of God' to Jesus; that is a language which began to be used only by Christians influenced by Greek culture. It is the language used by Saint Paul, as it was used by the author of the fourth Gospel, and both found in it deep meanings, sufficiently clear to them" (p. 39). And: "We believe it probable that Jesus thought of himself as 'the servant of God' and presented himself to people in that capacity. The Hebrew word 'servant' is often translated into Greek by a word meaning both 'servant' and 'child', and the development of the word 'child' into the word 'son' is no difficult matter; but the concept of 'Son of God' sprang from the Greek world of thought" (p. 39, note 11). Then he says: "At one period the matter became confused for some believers who did not know Aramaic well, for the expression 'son of man' in that language means simply 'a human being' or 'a man'. It seemed to these believers that this expression, which they also met in the collection of sayings known as the 'Logia', must contain a deep mystery... and so they decided that 'Son of Man' was a special Christian synonym for the word 'Messiah'. Analysis of the texts confirms the error of those who took this view in interpreting the expression" (p. 40).

I say, nevertheless: even if we take these words at face value, according to the texts of the Gospels, they do not, by their context, their shared usage and the way in which they are used, establish the claim of Jesus's divinity or any distinction in his human nature. The author.

{101}------------------------------------------------

## The second matter: the specific address of Jesus's mission to the Children of Israel

Jesus (peace be upon him) defined that specific address himself, just as he defined it for his pupils and disciples. As for the texts in all the Gospels in which Jesus speaks to his disciples and which point clearly to his having been charged with announcing a universal and final message for a messenger to come after him, I have chosen not to cite them, because their interpretation is open to dispute (35).

On the evidence of these texts, and from a reading of the four Gospels, we can support, to some extent, the view of Charles Guignebert, who says that Jesus (peace be upon him) "did not bring a new religion, nor even any new rites of worship; he brought only a unique personal conception of piety within the framework of the Jewish religion, a religion whose beliefs, law and rites he never claimed to wish to change" (36).

---

(35) See Matthew, chapters 24 and 25; Mark, chapter 13; Luke, chapters 12-21;
John, chapters 5-15.
(36) Al-Masihiyya: Nash'atuha wa Tatawwuruha (Christianity: Its Origin and Development), p. 48.

{102}------------------------------------------------

## The disciples and the apostles

We have given this question a separate discussion because examining it differs from examining the corresponding question in Judaism. While the religious texts of all three religions accept that messengers and prophets followed one another after Moses (peace be upon him), the existence of messengers and prophets after Jesus (peace be upon him) is denied by the Jews, who do not recognise the mission of Jesus (peace be upon him), let alone disciples or apostles after him. Likewise, the prevailing belief among Muslims is that no messenger or prophet came after Jesus (peace be upon him) until the Seal of the Messengers, Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him). The claim that prophethood and revelation continued among the followers of Jesus (peace be upon him) is therefore something that Jews and Muslims agree in denying. Nor does it rest on any evidence worth considering. This question thus belongs to the domain of *nihla* (a claim or doctrine put forward by human beings) and assertion, even though Christians regard it as belonging to the domain of *milla* (religion as God revealed it) and faith.

## The disciples

They are the companions of Jesus (peace be upon him): the first to believe in him, to become his pupils and to help him in his mission. The Qur'an and the Gospels agree on this, except that the Qur'an does not name or count them, whereas the Gospels take care to give their names and number, though they differ among themselves on this. They also differ about the disciples' mission, to which the Qur'an does not refer: while Mark states that Christ sent them out into the world, Matthew states that Christ forbade them to go into foreign lands. We shall give their names on the basis of Matthew's account, since he alone lists them in a separate passage and since his is the first Gospel in the usual order, bearing in mind the differences we have pointed out. Luke, for example, omits "Lebbaeus" and gives in his place another name not mentioned by Matthew, "Judas the brother of James"; and John alone, among the Gospels, mentions two others, named "Nathanael and Philip", and so on.

Matthew says:

> "Then he (that is, Christ, peace be upon him) called his twelve disciples and gave them authority over unclean spirits, to cast them out and to heal every disease and every infirmity.
>
> Now the names of the twelve apostles are these:
> Simon, who is called Peter, and Andrew his brother; James the son of Zebedee,

{103}------------------------------------------------

> and John his brother; Philip and Bartholomew; Thomas and Matthew the tax collector; James the son of Alphaeus, and Lebbaeus, surnamed Thaddaeus; Simon the Cananaean, and Judas Iscariot, who betrayed him." (37)

### The apostles

There are the seventy apostles whom Christ is said to have sent out to teach Christianity. And there are the hundred and twenty whom Peter addressed in a speech, after which they were distinguished by the Holy Spirit and went out to call people to Christianity. None of these is mentioned in the Qur'an.

Ibn Hazm, in his book *Al-Fasl fi al-Milal* (The Decisive Treatise on the Religions), refuses to accept that these are the disciples of Jesus of whom the Qur'an speaks, and he grounds his view in the corruption of their beliefs about Christ. He says: "Let every Muslim know that these whom the Christians name, and claim were disciples of Christ (peace be upon him), such as Peter and Matthew, were never believers at all, let alone disciples. Rather they were liars who made light of God Most High: either they affirmed the divinity of Christ (peace be upon him), believed it and went to extremes about him, as the Saba'iyya and the other extremist sects went to extremes about Ali (may God be pleased with him); or they were planted by the Jews, as the Jews themselves claim, to corrupt the religion of the followers of Christ (peace be upon him) and lead them astray. As for the disciples whom God praised, they are truly the friends of God ... and we do not know their names, for God Most High has not named them for us" (38).

Ibn Hazm's denial, though it has some basis, does not rest on strong grounds, since these Gospels may have been falsely attributed to the disciples, or alteration may have overtaken them after their time. Indeed, holding that the names are authentic while their sayings were altered is the easier position by the measure of scholarly criticism, since names are less prone to alteration than the sayings attributed to them, being easy to remember and to hand down by widespread transmission. A firm judgement here is therefore left to scholarly investigation of these questions, and that investigation has not yet reached any decisive conclusion on this matter.

(37) The Gospel of Matthew, chapter 10.

(38) [Vol.] 3, pp. 38-39.

{104}------------------------------------------------

## Their scriptures

As for the scripture revealed to Jesus (peace be upon him), it is the Gospel (*Injil*), as the Noble Qur'an states, where God Most High says of Jesus:

> And He will teach him the Book and wisdom, and the Torah and the Gospel. (39)

The revealed Gospel did not contain many legal rulings on the lawful and the unlawful. Rather, it centred on matters of belief and morals which Christ (peace be upon him) proclaimed in order to free the Israelites from their burdens of sin and their alterations. We know nothing reliable about the revealed Gospel as an authentic religious document; the hand of alteration and substitution has reached it. The Jews took this as a justification for their disbelief in Christ, since in their view he had been commanded to follow Moses (peace be upon him) and the Torah, yet he departed from it in certain matters, among them replacing the Sabbath with Sunday, permitting the eating of pork, dispensing with circumcision, and other rulings (40).

The Noble Qur'an has depicted the state of the Jews and the Christians after they had altered the Torah and the Gospel, and their mutual accusations, in His words:

> The Jews say, "The Christians stand on nothing," and the Christians say, "The Jews stand on nothing," though they both recite the Book. (41)

Moreover, we do not know whether the Gospel revealed to Jesus was written down by Jesus himself or dictated by him to someone else. What we have in our hands now, however, are those Gospels which are regarded as accounts

(41) Al-Baqara: 113.

(39) Al Imran: 48.

(40) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya (Comparative Religion: Christianity), p. 174.

{105}------------------------------------------------

of Jesus (peace be upon him) and his teachings. For this reason no Christian today claims that the four Gospels now in existence were dictated to Jesus by revelation; rather, they were composed by four men, who composed them at different dates.

The Christians' Holy Scripture comprises two parts:

(a) The Old Testament, containing the Torah and the religious books of the Jews as we set them out in the previous chapter.

(b) The New Testament, comprising the four Gospels and the Acts of the Apostles, together with their teachings, their history and other material from the time of Jesus and after.

The Christians, however, accuse the Jews of altering the Torah by interpreting it and adding commentaries and glosses to it. The Christians therefore resorted to interpreting the Torah in the light of their Gospels, so as to serve their belief about Christ and his divinity. Hence the New Testament became the Christians' more important book.

Some researchers hold that the word "*Injil*" goes back to a Greek root meaning the reward given to one who brings good tidings, and that it was then used for the good tidings themselves. Jesus used it in the sense of "the good tidings of salvation". This usage continued and was extended until it became a title for the teachings of Christ which he proclaimed for the salvation of man from sin.

The name then settled as a title for the Christians' holy books, so that one came to say the Gospel of Matthew, the Gospel of Luke, and so on (42).

The New Testament contains twenty-seven books, which can be classified under three divisions:

First: the historical books.

These tell of the life of Jesus (peace be upon him) and his disciples. This division comprises the four Gospels.

Second: the didactic books.

These are the letters that the first teachers of Christianity sent to various regions, calling people in them to Christianity.

(42) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, p. 174.

{106}------------------------------------------------

## Third: the Revelation of John the Divine

It tells of a waking vision of John concerning Christ and his mission, and of Christ's continuing contact with the men of the Christian religion.

The New Testament, with the Gospels it contains and in its present form, is regarded as the final version approved by the Church in 325 CE, after bloody disputes among the Christians themselves over the authenticity of their religious books.

At this point we wish to draw attention to an important and significant matter: the New Testament in its present form is the work of certain Christian clergy and the expression of a particular point of view. It is a formulation of a particular creed or doctrine that had spread among the Christians before the date of its composition, namely the doctrine of the Trinity and of the divinity of Jesus, held by Paul and his followers and later adopted by the Church for reasons we shall mention below.

It is clear from this that the Christians' present holy books were composed, or became authoritative and acquired official status, about four centuries after Christ. On this point we cite the view of a priest who embraced Islam, named Abdul Ahad Dawud, who says:

> "These twenty-seven books or letters, composed by eight writers, were not officially admitted as a whole into the number of the holy books until the fourth century, by the endorsement and ruling of the general Council of Nicaea in 325 CE.
>
> Therefore none of these letters was accepted and authenticated by the Church and the whole Christian world before that date.
>
> Then there came to Nicaea, from the Christian communities in the various parts of the globe, more than a thousand spiritual envoys, bringing with them dozens of Gospels and hundreds of letters for scrutiny. There the four Gospels were selected from more than forty or fifty Gospels, and the twenty-one letters were selected from countless letters, and they were ratified.
>
> The body that chose the 'New Testament' was the body that affirmed the divinity of Christ; or rather, the selection of the books of the New Testament was made on the basis of rejecting the

{107}------------------------------------------------

> Christian books that contained teachings not in agreement with the creed of 'Nicaea', and burning them all." (43)

From this it is clear that the source of the Christians' beliefs is not Christ himself, nor the revelation sent down to him, but the Church and the Christian clergy long after Jesus (peace be upon him).

The source of Christianity in its beliefs is therefore in truth a human source, not a divine one as they claim; and this is the view of trustworthy scholars among the Christians themselves.

Here, with regard to our purpose of distinguishing *milla* from *nihla* in Christianity, through its own documents (that is, the New Testament), through its origin and development, and through the continuing critical studies of its heritage, we find ourselves in a clearer position, and one that makes this purpose less laborious to achieve, than our position with regard to Judaism. It is no rash venture for any scholarly inquiry, relying on those studies, to say that Christianity in its present form, and with the original documents now in our hands, is a *nihla* both in its content and in the attribution of that content. Nothing of the meaning of *milla* remains in it except the historical aspect, which, for now at least, we have no way of knowing reliably except by relying on the Noble Qur'an.

The Christians differ from the Jews in the claim of an unbroken chain of transmission, since they are all agreed that the Gospels in their hands are not the Gospel revealed to Jesus (peace be upon him). Indeed, they all pass over any mention of that Gospel. Rather, these are biographies or histories composed by four men, some of whom were his contemporaries and some not, at different times, the largest of them no more than twenty-eight pages in length. The first of them is:

#### The Gospel of Matthew

Matthew, as they say, was one of the disciples and a companion of Jesus (peace be upon him). He died in Abyssinia in 79 CE, having made it the base for spreading his mission. He composed his Gospel nine years after Jesus (peace be upon him) was raised up, and wrote it in Aramaic, or in Hebrew as Ibn Hazm says; but that copy was lost and no trace of it has been found.

Then a book appeared in Greek which was said to be a translation of the Gospel of Matthew, and it is of this that we have copies now. But neither its translator nor the date of its translation is known, which casts doubt on

(43) Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, p. 174.

{108}------------------------------------------------

the soundness of its attribution to Matthew. And we know that any doubt about a religious source lessens its value as a document to be relied on in belief and in the fundamentals of religion.

### Then the Gospel of Mark

It was composed, as they say, by Mark the Harunite, pupil of Simon Cephas son of Thomas [sic], called Peter, the pupil of Christ (peace be upon him). He composed it twenty-two years after Christ was raised up, writing it in Greek while he was in Antioch. Mark made Egypt the base of his mission and was killed there in 62 CE.

The Christian sources are confused about the soundness of its attribution to him. While some sources assert that it was written after 62 CE, other sources assert that Mark was killed in Egypt in 62 CE. How, then, can the Gospel date from after the death of its author?

They also disagree as to whether Mark wrote his Gospel himself, or dictated it to someone else, or whether it was composed by one of his pupils on the basis of sayings he had received orally from his teacher.

Others hold that Peter, the chief of the disciples and Mark's teacher, was the one who related it from his pupil. This is implausible and unusual: how could the matter be reversed, with a teacher relating from his pupil? There are also those who hold that it, and the Gospel of John, were composed by Paul (44).

### The Gospel of Luke

Luke was neither one of the disciples nor one of their pupils; he was a pupil of Paul, and Paul never saw Christ. That is, he belongs to the followers of the followers. He composed his Gospel in Greek in Antioch, in the lands of the Byzantines.

### And the Gospel of John

It is the most obscuring of them on the question of the divinity of Christ. Researchers consider it most likely that the Gospel attributed to John was composed by one of the later Christians after Christ, and not by John the disciple, as is mistakenly supposed. The Encyclopaedia Britannica states:

---

(44) Injil Barnaba: Dirasat hawla Wahdat al-Din (The Gospel of Barnabas: Studies on the Unity of Religion), p. 35, edited by Sayf Allah Ahmad Fadil, Dar al-Qalam, Kuwait, 2nd ed., 1983.

{109}------------------------------------------------

> "As for the Gospel of John, it is without any doubt or question a forged book, by which its author sought to set two of the disciples against each other, namely Saint John and Saint Matthew. This forging writer claimed in the book itself that he was the disciple whom Christ loved, and the Church took this sentence at face value and declared that the writer was John the disciple, and put his name on the book explicitly, although its author was certainly someone other than John. This book is no different from certain books of the Torah which have no connection with those to whom they are attributed.
>
> We feel pity and compassion for those who spend their utmost effort to link, by even the flimsiest tie, that philosophical man who composed this book in the second generation with the disciple John, the Galilean fisherman, for their labours are lost to them in vain, since they grope without guidance." (45)

It seems that Ibn Hazm fell into the same error, attributing this Gospel to John the pupil of Christ; and Ibn Hazm's own words confirm that error of falsely attributing this Gospel to John the disciple. He says:

> "And the fourth: a history composed by John son of Zebedee, one of the pupils of Christ, some sixty-odd years after Christ was raised up, and he wrote it in Greek ... and this same John is the one who translated the Gospel of his companion Matthew from Hebrew into Greek." (46)

It is clear from Ibn Hazm's statement that John composed it more than sixty years after Christ was raised up. That is a long period: it is hardly conceivable that John should have been a companion of Christ and then lived more than sixty years after him, composing his Gospel only after that, especially as Ibn Hazm says that John died of poison.

Nor is it reasonable that John should have composed his Gospel at an age of more than a hundred years, if we take it that John was over forty years old when Christ was raised up.

Al-Shahrastani, in *Al-Milal wa al-Nihal* (Religions and Sects), seems to have fallen into the same error, for he said: "Then four of the disciples gathered, and each of them compiled a collection which he called

(45) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, pp. 180 ff.

(46) Al-Fasl fi al-Milal, vol. 2/3.

{110}------------------------------------------------

the Gospel; they were Matthew, Luke, Mark and John" (47). Perhaps he and Ibn Hazm both took over the Christians' account at face value.

This Gospel, then, was either composed by a Christian man named John, and the similarity of names is what led historians into this confusion, as the Encyclopaedia Britannica says; or it was falsely attributed to John the disciple in order to promote the ideas it contains.

From this we see that the Gospels, to say nothing of the other letters, were all written from memory, after the time of the events they relate and outside the place where those events occurred. This explains the variation and contradiction in their narration of the facts of those events. All of this makes the question of the chain of transmission and the text, in the case of Christianity, beside the point, calling for no discussion or debate.

---

(47) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal, 1/202.

{111}------------------------------------------------

## Their sects

Christianity, as we have already mentioned, passed through many phases, and its sects multiplied in step with the development of its beliefs, the substitution and alteration that befell them, and the disagreement over the nature of Christ (peace be upon him). Some of them died out and some perished, until Christianity reached its present form.

Since the church councils are in reality the true authors of the Christian religion in its present form, which differs entirely from what was brought by the founder of the religion, Jesus (peace be upon him); and since the first council to lay the foundations of the Christian creed was the Council of Nicaea in 325 CE, we shall take this date as the dividing line between two eras in the history of the sects.

There were sects that existed before it or rebelled against it, but they were fought and their adherents hunted down to the death, because they said of Christ something close to the truth, such as:

1. The followers of Paul of Samosata, who was a patriarch in Antioch before the appearance of [official] Christianity and then embraced Christianity. He held something close to what Arius later held: that Jesus was the servant of God and His messenger, a prophet like one of the prophets, whom God created in his mother's womb without a male, so that he is a human being like all other human beings.

2. The followers of Arius, who was a priest in Alexandria in the reign of Constantine I, around the beginning of the fourth century CE. He upheld the unity of God and held that Jesus was a created servant of God.

3. The followers of Macedonius, who was a patriarch in Constantinople after the appearance of Christianity, in the reign of Constantius II.

He held that Jesus was a created servant, a human prophet like the other prophets, whom God sent to guide the Children of Israel; and that he is the Holy Spirit and the Word of God, the Spirit and the Word being both created by God (48).

(48) See Al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa' wa al-Nihal, 1/48.

{112}------------------------------------------------

In any case, all these sects and others like them have died out, and whoever believes as they did is no longer counted among the Christians today, because the Christian creed has settled on belief in the Trinity, or the divinity of Jesus. That creed has been professed by all Christians from the Council of Nicaea in 325 CE until today. The differences and multiplicity of sects now revolve around disputed points concerning the nature of Christ. The best known of these sects are:

### First: the Melkites

Of them al-Shahrastani says: "They are the followers of Malka, who appeared in the land of the Byzantines and took control of it"<sup>(49)</sup>; and that was in the fifth century CE.

### Their teachings

They hold that God is a nature composed of three things: Father, Son and Holy Spirit. They are thus the people of the Trinity, and perhaps they are the ones meant by the words of God Most High [Al-Ma'ida 5:73]:

> They have indeed disbelieved who say, "God is the third of three." (49)

They said that Jesus is perfect God and perfect man, and that Mary gave birth to this God-man whom they call Christ.

This belief was adopted by the Western Church (the Catholics).

### Second: the Nestorians

So called after Nestorius, who was Patriarch of Constantinople in 431 CE.

They hold the same view as the Melkites, except that they say Mary did not give birth to God but gave birth to the man, since the created does not give birth to the Creator. Mary thus gave birth to a man whom the divine nature took on after his birth.

### Third: the Jacobites

So called after a well-known preacher named Jacob Baradaeus.

(49) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal, 1/203.

{113}------------------------------------------------

They said that Christ is God Most High Himself, incarnate in the form of a man; that it was he who was crucified and killed; that the world remained for three days without anyone to govern it; and that he then rose and returned exactly as he had been.

Of them the Noble Qur'an informed us in the words of God Most High [Al-Ma'ida 5:17]:

> They have indeed disbelieved who say, "God is the Messiah, son of Mary."

In their view Christ is perfect God, whom Mary bore; for this reason they call her the Mother of God.
This doctrine was adopted by the Eastern Church (the Orthodox) (50).

---

(50) Al-Fasl fi al-Milal, 1/48; Al-Milal wa al-Nihal, 1/203-207; and Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya,
pp. 165-166.

{114}------------------------------------------------

## Christianity as the Church shaped it: beliefs and rites

Christianity contains no legal rulings; the most important thing in it is the Christians' belief about Jesus (peace be upon him). We must therefore now trace the origin and development of that belief through the church councils, which, as we have said, are the true authors of the Christian religion.

We do not stray from the truth if we say that Christianity in its present form is a man-made religion, not a divine one, in that it has settled on beliefs and rites that set it apart not only from Islam, which Moses and Jesus brought, but even from Judaism and from Christianity as we see them now in the Torah and the four Gospels. "For the indisputable truth is that the Church was able to triumph during the fourth century only thanks to the defeat of the first faith, which we may call the faith of the Twelve" (51).

As for the beliefs:

They consist of two pillars:

The first: the divinity of Jesus, or the doctrine of the Trinity.

The first Christians held the correct belief about Jesus: that he was the servant of God and His messenger, whom God sent to guide the Children of Israel. But a short while after Christ was raised up, the very nature of Christ's life, the extraordinary circumstances of his birth and his end, and the miracles that appeared at his hands stirred up debate about his nature. This debate was provoked and led astray by alien elements, which exploited this unique nature of Jesus (peace be upon him), spread strange ideas and false beliefs, and made them attractive to people, until Christianity ended as a religion entirely different from the one the first Christians had professed.

The first to proclaim the divinity of Christ was a man named Saul, later called Saint Paul. He was a Jew, fiercely hostile to Christianity, relentless in persecuting

(51) Al-Masihiyya: Nash'atuha wa Tatawwuruha, p. 190.

{115}------------------------------------------------

the Christians and inciting others against them. But he suddenly changed his religion and his ways, and embraced Christianity in 38 CE.

Let us give a brief account of his life, from what Luke said about him in the Acts of the Apostles, which shows us how intense his hatred and open his hostility were towards Christ and his mission. He says:

> "As for Saul, he was ravaging the church, entering house after house, and finding men and women he committed them to prison." (52)

As for his sudden conversion and entry into Christianity, proclaiming the divinity of Jesus (peace be upon him) and claiming that this teaching had been revealed to him, Luke says:

> "As for Saul, he was still breathing out threats and slaughter against the disciples of the Lord. He went to the high priest and asked him for letters to the congregations in Damascus, so that if he found any belonging to the Way, men or women, he might bring them bound to Jerusalem.
>
> And as he journeyed, it happened that as he drew near to Damascus, suddenly a light from heaven flashed around him, and he fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him: Saul, Saul, why do you persecute me?!
>
> He said: Who are you, sir?
>
> And the Lord said: I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting ...
>
> And trembling and bewildered he said: Lord, what do you want me to do?
>
> And the Lord said to him: Rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you must do ...
>
> Saul rose from the ground, and though his eyes were open he saw no one.
>
> So they led him by the hand and brought him into Damascus, and for three days he was without sight, and neither ate nor drank.
>
> Now there was a disciple at Damascus named Ananias ... and the Lord said to him: Rise and go to the street ... and inquire at the house of Judas for a man of Tarsus named Saul, for behold, he is praying.
>
> But Ananias answered: Lord, I have heard from many about this man, how much evil he has done to your saints in Jerusalem.
>
> But the Lord said to him: Go, for he is a chosen vessel of mine to carry my name before the Gentiles

(52) Acts of the Apostles, chapter 8.

{116}------------------------------------------------

> and kings and the Children of Israel ...
>
> So Ananias departed and entered the house, and laying his hands on him he said: Brother Saul, the Lord Jesus, who appeared to you on the road, has sent me ... And immediately something like scales fell from his eyes, and he regained his sight at once ...
>
> And Saul was with the disciples in Damascus for some days, and immediately he began to proclaim Christ in the synagogue, that he is the Son of God." (53)

This is a brief account of Saul, or Saint Paul, as Luke relates it.

Researchers and historians relate that Paul was highly intelligent and keenly interested in the study of ancient religions and philosophies, and that he carried much of them over into Christianity. He taught people that Jesus was not only the promised Messiah but the Son of God, who came down to earth to offer himself as a sacrifice, crucified in atonement for the sin of mankind committed by their father Adam; his death was thus a great sacrifice by a great god for the salvation of mankind.

Saint Paul set about bringing his doctrine closer to his pupils' understanding, giving them the example of the ancient Egyptian beliefs: that Jesus was like Osiris, a god who died to be raised to life anew and to grant people immortality.

Thus Paul entered Christianity and became its teacher; indeed, in the view of Western researchers he is the true founder of Christianity in its present form (54).

Paul himself claims that what he preaches and proclaims is neither from himself nor from any other person, but that he received it by revelation from Jesus Christ. In this way he blocks the path to any objection to, or discussion of, what he says.

He says:

> "I make known to you, brothers, the gospel that I preached, that it is not according to man; for I did not receive it from man, nor was I taught it, but through a revelation of Jesus Christ." (55)

(53) Acts of the Apostles, chapter 9.

(54) Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, p. 90.

(55) To the Galatians, chapter 1.

{117}------------------------------------------------

And he says of himself:

> "Paul, an apostle, not from men nor through a man, but [through] Jesus Christ." (56)

Through this infallibility, or divine connection, which Paul conferred on himself, he introduced into Christianity four principal doctrines, which became the basis that the church councils adopted and decreed as the final creed of the Christians thereafter. These doctrines are:

1. Christianity is not a religion for the Jews alone, but a universal religion.

2. The Trinity: the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit are one God; that is, the divinity of Christ.

3. The crucifixion of Jesus was an atonement for the sin of mankind.

4. Jesus is the one who will judge mankind on the Day of Resurrection: "For he rose from the dead three days after his crucifixion and ascended to heaven to sit at the right hand of his Father, as he had been before, in order to judge between people on the Day of Resurrection" (57).

Two factors helped to spread Paul's ideas and doctrines among people and to bring the Christians to embrace them:

### The first factor

A psychological cause connected with Paul himself. He announced his conversion to Christianity after having been one of its fiercest opponents, and this had an effect that revived the hopes of the persecuted Christians for the triumph of their cause and for their deliverance. This psychological factor, together with Paul's intelligence and cunning, did much to win acceptance for his ideas and spread his mission, and it became the driving force that carried his doctrine to dominance over the other doctrines in the hearts of the masses.

### The second factor

The persecutions that consumed many of Christianity's original sources and destroyed the true Christians after Jesus was raised up, and the continuation of this persecution with violence at the hands of the Jews and the Roman emperors, made people believe the myths that revived their hopes and stiffened their resolve, and accept death and torture in imitation of the Son of God who was crucified as a ransom for mankind.

(56) To the Galatians, chapter 1.

(57) See Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, p. 95.

{118}------------------------------------------------

We must now point to the most important of the councils that codified Christianity and gave it its religious legitimacy, in respect of its books, rituals and rites:

### The Council of Nicaea, 325 CE

During these centuries of persecution, which reached their peak in the age of the Emperor Diocletian (284-305 CE), called by the Christians the Age of the Martyrs because of how much Christian blood was shed in it, and amid the accompanying religious disputes among the Christians themselves over the ideas and beliefs about Christ that Paul had spread, Christianity lost its true foundations and character. Many philosophical ideas and popular superstitions alike entered it, leaving it a mixture of contradictions and superstitions, especially concerning the nature of Christ, about which opinions and doctrines multiplied.

Some said: he is a man like other people, and a messenger like other messengers.
Others said that he is God or the son of God, or both together.

The Church feared that these disagreements would destroy Christianity itself, especially after it had become the religion of the Roman state in the reign of Constantine, who had embraced Christianity as Paul taught it, and who feared the disintegration of the empire because of these disputes. For this reason Constantine assembled the patriarchs and bishops in what is called the Council of Nicaea in 325 CE, to lay down a single creed for all Christians to profess.

The council met for the first time with 2,048 members, representing the churches and clergy in all parts of the empire.

They then began to discuss the various beliefs and opinions about Christ, especially what Arius the Egyptian had declared: that the Father alone is God and that the Son is made by God. Before long the council split between supporters and opponents of Arius's teaching, and it could not reach a unified decision on the nature of Christ. The emperor then decided to impose by himself the doctrine of the Trinity in which he believed. To that end he had Arius and many of his supporters within the council killed and the rest exiled, and then called another council in the same place and the same year, attended only by those who upheld the Trinity and the divinity of Christ, numbering 318 members (58).

(58) The previous reference, pp. 168-170.

{119}------------------------------------------------

They arrived at their decision, which is regarded as the basis of the Christian creed to this day. This is the text of the decision:

> "We believe in the one God, the Father, Master of all things, Maker of what is seen and what is unseen; and in the one Son, Jesus Christ, the only Son of God, the firstborn of all creation, who was born of his Father before all worlds, and is not made;
>
> true God, of the substance of his Father, by whose hand the worlds were perfected and all things created; for our sake
>
> and for the sake of humankind, and for our salvation, he came down from heaven, was incarnate of the Holy Spirit and became man, was conceived and born of the Virgin Mary, was killed and crucified in the days of Pilate, and was buried;
>
> then he rose on the third day, ascended to heaven and sat at the right hand of his Father; and he is ready to come again to judge between the dead and the living. And we believe in the one Holy Spirit, the Spirit of truth, who proceeds from his Father; and in one baptism for the forgiveness of sins; and in one holy Christian community; and in the resurrection of our bodies and in life everlasting, for ever and ever."<sup>(59)</sup>

By this decision Christ became, for the Christians, God or the son of God, and the Christian creed came to rest on the idea of the three hypostases, or the Trinity.

After that, councils followed one another to decree doctrinal principles concerning Christ and the men of the Church, such as:

**The Council of Rome, 869 CE**

It decreed:

1. The Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son.

2. Whoever seeks judgement in a matter relating to religion shall bring his case to the Church of Rome.

3. Christians in all parts of the world are subject to the decisions of the head of the Church of Rome.

(59) Al-Milal wa al-Nihal, 1/204.

{120}------------------------------------------------

## The Council of Constantinople, 879

It decreed that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone and not from the Son. This was regarded as contrary to the earlier Council of Rome and as a breach with the Church of Rome.

With this council the Church split into Western and Eastern, each with its own council, which does not recognise the decisions of the other.

## The Council of Rome, 1225

It decreed that the Church possesses forgiveness and grants it to whomever it wishes.

## Then the Council of Rome, 1869

It decreed that the Pope is infallible and has the right to legislate, that nothing he issues may be contradicted, and that whoever contradicts it is an unbeliever and accursed.

In decreeing this authority for itself, the Church relied on certain religious texts which claim that Christ gave his pupils the power to work miracles, and that this power was then inherited by the clergy after them; and that revelation did not cease when Jesus was raised up but continues among his pupils and their followers for as long as God wills.

The first to plant this idea and spread it in Christian thought was Paul, as we have seen. Anyone who looks through the four Gospels and the books of the New Testament, especially the Acts of the Apostles and the letters of Paul, will find the idea of the angel who guides the believers and communicates with them, and of the miracles that appeared at the hands of Christ's pupils, recurring often and with remarkable insistence.

The men of religion, or of the Church, thus have among the Christians the same sanctity and infallibility as Jesus Christ; hence rebellion against obedience to the Church and its men is disbelief in Christ and in Christianity.

Likewise, the idea that revelation continued and came down upon the followers of Jesus is the justification on which the Christians rely in accepting the New Testament and believing what it contains, even though they all admit that it was neither composed nor dictated by Jesus, but composed by his pupils.

## The second pillar: crucifixion and redemption

On this the Christians say that among God's attributes are justice and mercy.

{121}------------------------------------------------

By the attribute of justice, God had to punish mankind for the sin of their father Adam, who disobeyed and ate from the forbidden tree and was driven out of Paradise under God's wrath, he and his descendants after him, because of this sin.

By the attribute of mercy, God wished to forgive Adam and his descendants this sin.
There was no way to reconcile the two wills, or the two attributes of justice and mercy, except through the mediation of the Son of God, His only one. So He made him appear in the form of a man, living a man's life, and then to be crucified unjustly in atonement for the sin of mankind; or, in another version, he took on this task himself.

This is the text of what appears in the New Testament:

> "The Son of Man has come to save what was lost. Through His love and mercy He made a way of salvation; therefore Christ is the one who atones for the sins of the world, and he is the mediator who reconciled the love of God Most High with His justice and mercy.
>
> For justice required that people should continue to be far from God because of what their father had committed. But by the joining of justice and mercy, and through the mediation of the only Son and his acceptance of atonement for the sins of creation, people were brought near to the Lord after being far away." (60)

And in the Gospel of John, chapter three, it says:

> "He loved the world so much that He gave His only Son, so that whoever believes in him should not perish but have eternal life. For God did not send His Son into the world to condemn the world, but that the world might be saved through him."

Here an unavoidable question arises: how can the sin of Adam, who disobeyed God, be forgiven through the sin of the son of Adam who killed the Son of God?! Or how can the sin of eating from the tree be forgiven through a sin far more hideous, namely the killing of the Son of God by the son of Adam?!

Further, if the essence of Christ's message is that God is love, and forbearance to the point that if someone strikes you on the right cheek you turn the left to him also, would it not have been more fitting for the very fountain of love and forbearance to forgive Adam and his descendants without resorting to this harsh trial of theirs,

(60) Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya, pp. 135-136.

{122}------------------------------------------------

and to plunging them into a sin that cannot be atoned for? For how can whoever kills the Son of God deserve any forbearance or forgiveness?!

This is the Christian creed as the Church shaped it and as Christians profess it today; and we praise God Most High for guiding us to Islam.

### The rites

These are rituals that do not rise in importance to the doctrinal principles mentioned above. They differ from them in that a person is not a Christian until he believes in those principles, whereas a person is a Christian even if he does not perform the rites, though he is then regarded as disobedient or sinful. The most important of these rites as the Church laid them down are:

### Baptism

Baptism is a rite the Christians took from the Jews. It is stated in the Gospel of John, chapter one, that John used to baptise people in the River Jordan, and that it was he who baptised Christ (peace be upon him); for this reason he was called "John the Baptist". It used to be enough for a person to testify that Jesus, the crucified one, is the Messiah, who died in atonement and rose in atonement for the sins of mankind, and who will return to judge between the living and the dead and establish the kingdom of God; after this profession he would be baptised by immersion in water three times with the help of a brother in the faith. This rite then developed along with the development of the church order, until it came to be performed with complicated rituals, prayers and offerings.

Baptism is indispensable for every Christian. It is performed by a priest of the church to which the baptised person belongs, and no one other than a priest performs it except in necessity, in which case it is called "emergency baptism". For them it is a holy obligation that leads to the purification of the soul and its release from sin, and a proof of the baptised person's faith and obedience to the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit as his God and sole object of worship. Its form is that the priest sprinkles water on the forehead of the person being baptised, or dips part of his body, or his whole body, in water three times: the first in the name of the Father, the second in the name of the Son and the third in the name of the Holy Spirit, together with the laying on of hands, which is accompanied by anointing with the holy oil.

As for its timing, the Christians have not agreed among themselves. Some hold that its time is childhood; some hold that it is an obligation whose time is open, meaning that it may validly be performed at any time in a person's life; and some hold that it is best performed at the point of death, on the grounds that

{123}------------------------------------------------

baptism removes misdeeds and wipes away sins, so it is better done at the end of life, when no sins will follow it<sup>(61)</sup>.

### The Eucharist, or Communion

This rite began as a gathering of the brethren to eat pieces of bread together as an expression of their brotherhood in faith. It then developed until it became a symbol with complex philosophical rituals of its own, in which the devout, amid hymns and prayers, gather around a table bearing bread and wine that represent Christ and his blood; those assembled partake of them so as to be united bodily and spiritually with Christ. For individual believers it takes the form of the person receiving from the priest's hand a piece of bread and a little water or wine. It symbolises the last supper of Jesus with his disciples, as the Gospels relate it, when he gave them bread and wine: the bread a symbol of the body of Christ, which was broken as a ransom for mankind for its original sin, and the wine a symbol of his blood, shed for the same purpose.

When a person receives this communion from the hand of his priest, the bread and wine are transformed in the body of the communicant into the body and blood of Christ, so that a union takes place between the one who eats and Christ and his teachings.

In establishing this rite the Christians relied on the four Gospels. The Gospel of John, chapter [6:53-55], says:

> (Then Jesus said: Truly, truly I say to you, unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his blood, you have no life in you. Whoever eats my flesh and drinks my blood has eternal life, and I will raise him up on the last day... Whoever eats my flesh and drinks my blood abides in me and I in him. As the living Father sent me, and I live because of the Father, so whoever eats me will live because of me.)

The Gospel of Matthew [26:20-29] says of the last night of Christ: "When evening came, he reclined at table with the twelve... And while they were eating, Jesus took the bread, blessed it and broke it, gave it to the disciples and said: Take, eat; this is my body. And he took the cup, gave thanks and gave it to them, saying: Drink from it, all of you, for this is my blood of the

(61) Al-Masihiyya: Nash'atuha wa Tatawwuruha (Christianity: Its Origin and Development), pp. 48, 153.

{124}------------------------------------------------

new covenant, which is poured out for many for the forgiveness of sins. And I tell you, from now on I shall not drink of this fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new with you in the kingdom of my Father."

The Gospel of Luke, chapter [22:19-20], says of Christ:

> (And he took bread, gave thanks, broke it and gave it to them, saying: This is my body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of me. And likewise the cup after supper, saying: This cup is the new covenant in my blood, which is poured out for you.)

Likewise the Gospel of Mark [14:22-24] reads:

> (And while they were eating, Jesus took bread, blessed and broke it, and gave it to them, and said: Take, eat; this is my body. Then he took the cup, gave thanks and gave it to them, and they all drank from it, and he said to them: This is my blood of the new covenant, which is poured out for many.)

This is the rite of communion as the Christians hold it. Perhaps "Paul" was the first to lay down this rite, as he laid down others, for his letter to the Corinthians says:

> (The Lord Jesus, on the night he was betrayed, took bread, gave thanks, broke it and said: Take and eat; this is my body, broken for you; do this in remembrance. And likewise he gave them a little wine and said: Take and drink; this is my blood, shed for you. And whenever you eat this bread and drink this cup, you proclaim the Lord's death until he comes again.) (62)

The cross and the bearing of it:

The cross and the bearing of it are mentioned in the Gospel of Luke [9:23-24]:

> [And he said to all: If anyone wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross every day and follow me; for whoever wishes to save his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake will save it.]

From this came the Christians' veneration of the cross and their seeking of blessing by carrying it, as a symbol

(62) On the development of this rite see the previous reference, pp. 159-160.

{125}------------------------------------------------

of following the example of Christ (peace be upon him), who carried the cross on which he was crucified, and as an expression of disregard for life and readiness for sacrifice even unto death by crucifixion.

What adds further to the sanctity of the cross is that it was the instrument on which the body of Christ was stretched until death for the salvation of mankind; it is therefore the symbol that reminds every Christian how Christ suffered and sacrificed himself for his salvation.

### Confession and Repentance:

This is the rite established by the Council of Rome mentioned earlier. A Christian who wishes to repent of his sins must, for his repentance to be accepted, first confess all his sins before a priest, on condition that he hide none of them from him. After that the priest accepts his repentance and grants him the Lord's forgiveness, and he becomes as on the day his mother bore him.

The origins of this rite go back to Paul's doctrine that faith in the divinity of Christ and in his crucifixion, and not the works of the Law, is the ground for deserving salvation. This is the main note of his letters. In his letter to the Galatians he says [2:21]: "If righteousness comes through the Law, then Christ died for nothing"; and [3:13]: "Christ redeemed us from the curse of the Law by becoming a curse for us, for it is written: Cursed is everyone who is hanged on a tree"; [3:24-27]: "The Law was our tutor to bring us to Christ, so that we might be justified by faith. But now that faith has come we are no longer under a tutor, for you are all sons of God through faith in Christ Jesus, for all of you who were baptised into Christ have put on Christ"; and [4:4-5]: "God sent his Son, born under the Law, to redeem those who were under the Law."

On the other hand, the spiritual authority given to the priests as a continuation of the authority that Christ gave to Peter the disciple, "And I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven" (63), gave the priests and men of religion wide power over people in legislating, accepting and excommunicating. Because of its strangeness and the abuse made of it, this rite was the cause of the revolt of the German theologian (Martin Luther) in the sixteenth century, who took the indulgences as his justification for breaking with the Pope of Rome

(63) Matthew 16:19.

{126}------------------------------------------------

and founding the (Protestant) denomination, whose members set up a church of their own known as the Evangelical Church.

These indulgences became a winning card in the hand of the Church: "Whenever the Pope or a priest needed money for any purpose whatever, he had only to print a number of them and put them up for sale like stocks and bonds, with a blank space left on the certificate in which to write the name of whoever wished to exchange all his sins, past and future, for this wondrous certificate."

Since the fifteenth century many Christian theologians have recognised the moral and social danger of this rite, for it is nothing but a licence for whoever obtains it to commit whatever sins he pleases, as though the Church were declaring through these certificates that no disobedience harms a man who holds one, just as no obedience profits a man denied one.

So that the grave effect this rite can have on the individual and on society may become clear to you, here is the text of the certificate of indulgence:

> (May our Lord Jesus Christ have mercy on you, O ... [a blank is left in which to write the name of the purchaser], and absolve you by the merits of his most holy passion. And I, by the apostolic authority given to me, absolve you of all the punishments, judgements and ecclesiastical censures you have incurred; and also of all the excesses, sins and transgressions you have committed, however great and heinous they may be; and of every cause, even those reserved to our most Holy Father the Pope and the Apostolic See. I blot out all the stains of guilt and every mark of reproach you may have brought upon yourself on this occasion; I remit the punishments you would have been bound to endure in Purgatory; I restore you anew to communion in the sacraments of the Church and join you to the communion of the saints; I restore you again to the purity and righteousness that were yours at your baptism, so that at the hour of death the gate by which sinners enter the place of torment and punishment shall be shut before you, and the gate that leads to the paradise of joy shall be opened. And if you do not die for many long years, this grace shall remain unchanged until your last hour comes. In the name of the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit.) (64)

(64) Muqaranat al-Adyan: al-Masihiyya (Comparative Religion: Christianity), p. 146.

{127}------------------------------------------------

## The Gospel of Barnabas

Any discussion of this Gospel may be met by a question that arises in the mind: what is the scholarly benefit, or the purpose, of closing the treatment of Christianity with a discussion of a document that is disputed in both its attribution and its content? What is its value, at least for the two parties who have an important and direct relation to the subject, namely the one who accepts, out of faith or knowledge, that the Christian religion was altered, and the one who accepts its soundness out of faith and certainty? As a document surrounded by doubts, it cannot serve the first party as proof, nor can it bind the second or be used as proof against him.

Although this question poses a logical difficulty that has some force and merit, the justification I shall give may perhaps be an acceptable answer to it.

What led me to give attention to this Gospel at the close of the discussion of Christianity, despite my acknowledgement that the dispute over its attribution to Barnabas, or its place among the accepted Christian documents, has not yet been settled, is two things.

The first:

It concerns the method I have followed. Since I took the Qur'an, from a purely scholarly standpoint, as I indicated in the introduction to this study, as the criterion for criticism and for distinguishing between the *milla* (religion as God revealed it) and the *nihla* (a claim or doctrine put forward by human beings) in the three religions, I considered that one of the factors bearing on the assessment of the scholarly value of any document connected with those religions, as to error and correctness in attribution and content, or in chain of transmission and text, is the degree of its agreement or disagreement with the truths of the Noble Qur'an. On this view, if the Gospel of "Barnabas" is closer to the Qur'an than the other Christian documents, it has in my eyes more weight and scholarly value than those documents. Even if it is not distinguished from them or superior to them in the reliability of its chain of transmission and its historical attribution, it is without doubt distinguished from them and superior to them in the value of its text and its consistency with

{128}------------------------------------------------

the religious truths that the Qur'an set out. It will keep this distinction and superiority until it is proved, with certainty or with a preponderance admitting no doubt, that its attribution to "Barnabas", the disciple of Jesus (peace be upon him), is false. Only then can it be excluded as a Christian document to be relied on in writing the history of Christianity as milla and nihla. As far as I know, the exclusion of the Gospel of "Barnabas", from its appearance until now, is still carried out by the authority of the Church and not by the authority of scholarship.

### The second

Just as the value of this Gospel in terms of its historical attribution, what scholars call the "chain of transmission" (*sanad*), does not differ, in the balance of scholarly criticism, from the attribution of the documents recognised by the Church, those documents have, ever since the first century, remained open to criticism and to doubt about their historical value and their attribution to Christ (peace be upon him) and his disciples. Indeed, doubts about their scholarly value, in both chain and text, keep growing as the movement of scholarly and religious criticism grows and its methods advance among scholars, Christian scholars included.

My attention to the Gospel of Barnabas, whether as supporting evidence or as testimony, should therefore not be seen as a begging of the question, or as holding an opponent to a proof whose soundness he does not accept. It should rather be seen in the light of my aim of opening a kind of discussion on the subject of this study, through which I hope to provoke more consistent thinking, and to invite a pause, a rest for reflection and reconsideration of assumptions that may turn out to rest on no scholarly basis or sound religious logic. This aim, to which I alluded at the beginning of the discussion of Christianity, together with what we said under the first point, is, I think, sufficient to be a logical justification and an acceptable warrant for discussing the Gospel of "Barnabas".

Further: if, as the saying goes, the perfect crime has yet to be committed, then despite the efforts of the churchmen since the Council of Nicaea to crush every voice opposing the decisions they had reached, and despite their destruction of everyone who differed from them, whether a person or a book, the voice of truth is stronger than the voice of falsehood, however loud it may be.

For the Gospel of Barnabas appeared to bear witness to the truth on the one hand, and to the fabrication and alteration of the other Gospels on the other.

Barnabas was one of the disciples, as this Gospel states, or one of the apostles of Christ, as the "Acts of the Apostles" relates.

{129}------------------------------------------------

This Gospel was known in the early ages of Christianity, but it was among the religious books that the Church forbade to be read: in 492 CE Pope Gelasius I issued a decree listing the forbidden books, among them the Gospel of Barnabas.

Nevertheless this Gospel managed to escape destruction and to circulate secretly among some people, until a copy of it came to rest in the library of the royal court in Vienna, where it remains to this day.

At the beginning of the twentieth century this Gospel was translated into Arabic by Dr. Khalil Saadeh and published by Sayyid Muhammad Rida (65).

### Its teachings:

1 - Jesus denied his divinity and that he was the son of God, in the sight and hearing of a great multitude.

2 - The son whom Abraham resolved to offer as a sacrifice to God was Ishmael, not Isaac as the Jews claim.

3 - Jesus (peace be upon him) foretold the mission of Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him), mentioning Muhammad by name in many places in his Gospel, and said that when Adam was driven out of Paradise he saw lines above its gate in letters of light which read: "There is no god but God; Muhammad is the Messenger of God."

4 - Jesus was not crucified; the one crucified was Judas the traitor.

5 - Jesus upheld circumcision and forbade swine.

What concerns us now is to set out what Barnabas said about the matters that Paul introduced into Christianity and that the Church adopted as articles of belief, namely: **the doctrine of the Trinity, or the divinity of Jesus, and the doctrine of the crucifixion, or atonement**. Perhaps this was what moved Barnabas to write his Gospel, for he says in its preface:

> "Dearly beloved, the great and wonderful God has in these last days visited us through his prophet Jesus Christ in great mercy, by the teaching and the signs which Satan has made a pretext for leading many astray under pretence of piety, preaching a most impious doctrine, calling Christ son of

(65) See the introduction of the editor and translator to the Gospel of Barnabas, published by Dar al-Qalam, 2nd ed., Kuwait, 1983, which casts further light on, and adds to, what we have said in justification of discussing the Gospel of Barnabas. "The author"

{130}------------------------------------------------

> God, and rejecting the circumcision ordained of God for ever, permitting every unclean meat; among whom Paul also has been deceived, of whom I speak not without grief. And it is for this cause that I am writing down that truth which I have seen and heard in my companionship with Jesus, so that you may be saved and not be deceived by Satan and perish in the judgement of God. Therefore beware of everyone who preaches to you a new teaching contrary to what I write, that you may be saved eternally." (66)

### The Doctrine of the Trinity, or the Claim of Jesus' Divinity:

It is remarkable that the claim of Jesus' divinity appeared during his lifetime, and that Jesus himself refuted and disproved it and disowned those who spread it. What is remarkable is that falsehoods or religious legends about a messenger and his life usually appear after his time. With Jesus, however, because he was a wonder in his birth, in his miracles and in the close of his life on earth, together with the intellectual and religious climate, saturated with paganism and legend, in which Jesus (peace be upon him) appeared, all this made of Jesus (peace be upon him) a legend that he resisted during his lifetime. No sooner had he been raised up than it overwhelmed hearts and lorded it over minds, and whoever approached it with criticism or discussion was exposed to death and burning, or to mockery and derision. This was the lot of Barnabas, even though his Gospel is the most consistent of the Gospels with religious and rational logic alike. But such is the way of legend or superstition when it clouds insight, so that falsehood is seen as truth, or truth as falsehood.

Here are the events as Barnabas related them concerning the claim of Jesus' divinity and the factors that helped it spread.

### 1 - The Miracles of Jesus and Their Role in Spreading the Idea of His Divinity:

It is well known that Jesus (peace be upon him) was given many miracles, which the Qur'an mentions in the words of God Most High:

> And [We will make him] a messenger to the Children of Israel: "I have come to you with a sign from your Lord: I shall make for you out of clay the likeness of a bird, then breathe into it, and it will be a bird by God's leave;

{131}------------------------------------------------

> and I shall heal the blind from birth and the leper, and bring the dead to life by God's leave; and I shall tell you what you eat and what you store up in your houses. Surely in that is a sign for you, if you are believers." (67)

These miracles, in which the Qur'an agrees with the four Gospels, had their effect in captivating many people with Jesus and in leading them to believe that he was not human. The following text shows us how Jesus grew fearful of the effect of these miracles. Barnabas says:

> "In the second year of his prophetic office Jesus went down from Jerusalem and went to Nain. As he drew near to the gate of the city, the people of the city were carrying to the grave the only son of a widowed mother, and everyone was mourning him. When Jesus arrived, the people learned that the one who had come was Jesus, the prophet of Galilee. They therefore came forward and implored him on behalf of the dead man, asking him to raise him because he was a prophet, and his disciples did the same. Then Jesus was much afraid, and turning himself to God he said: Take me from the world, O Lord, for the world is mad, and they almost call me God. And having said this he wept." (68)

## 2 - Prevailing Beliefs:

Then the beliefs prevailing among the Jews and the Romans helped the claim of Jesus' divinity to spread and be accepted. Barnabas says: "The army of the Romans was at that time in Judaea, for our country was subject to them because of the sins of our forefathers. And it was the custom of the Romans to call anyone who did something new that benefited the people a god, and to worship him. So when some of these soldiers were in Nain, they rebuked one after another, saying: One of your gods has visited you and you pay no heed? Truly, if our gods visited us we would give them all we have; and you see how much we fear our gods, for we give their statues the best of what we have.

Satan whispered this manner of speech until he stirred up a tumult among the people of Nain. But Jesus did not stay in Nain; he turned to go to Capernaum. And the discord in Nain reached such a pitch that some said: The one who visited us is our God.

Others said: God is not seen; no one has seen him, not even Moses his servant; so he is not God, but rather the son of God.

(67) Al Imran 3:49.

(68) 47: 73-74.

{132}------------------------------------------------

Others said: He is neither God nor the son of God, for God has no body that he should beget; rather he is a great prophet from God" ... (69)

"And at this time there arose a great disturbance throughout all Judaea on account of Jesus, for the Roman soldiers, by the working of Satan, stirred up the Hebrews, saying that Jesus was God come to deliver them. Because of this a great sedition arose, so that all Judaea was in arms for forty days; son rose against father and brother against brother, for one party said that Jesus was God come into the world, another party said: No, he is a son; and others said: No, for God has no human likeness and therefore does not beget; rather Jesus of Nazareth is a prophet of God. This arose from the great signs that Jesus performed." (70)

### 3 - Jesus Himself Refutes the Claim of His Divinity:

"Truly I say to you, speaking from the heart, I shudder because the world will call me God, and I shall have to give an account for it. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, I am a mortal man like other men; for although God has set me as a prophet over the house of Israel for the health of the weak and the correction of sinners, I am the servant of God, and you are witnesses of this." (71)

Then Jesus answers the arguments of those who deified him because of the signs God had shown at his hands, saying: "Moses, with his rod, turned the sea to blood, the dust to lice, the dew to tempest and the light to darkness; he sent frogs and rats upon Egypt so that they covered the land; he slew the firstborn, divided the sea and drowned Pharaoh in it. I have done none of these things, and everyone acknowledges that Moses is now a dead man.

Joshua made the sun stand still and divided the Jordan, which I have not yet done, and everyone acknowledges that Joshua is now a dead man.
